ARCHITECTURE  /  MARKETING  /  OPERATIONS
WEBSITE
MASTER
BLUEPRINT
The reusable standard for building,
operating, and improving dynamic websites.
Owner-controlled CMS and blogs. Marketing automation and trustworthy conversion evidence. Search and advertising readiness. Secure, portable engineering and operations.
VERSION 1.3  |  7 OCTOBER 2026
Final consolidated edition • Implementation blueprint • Acceptance standard
Use with a completed site brief. Rankings, complete tracking, and zero maintenance are not guaranteed.
Read this first
A complete website is a working operating system for the business—not just a collection of attractive pages. This blueprint makes content editing, customer journeys, conversion evidence, data protection, and recovery part of the same delivery contract.
The default build
React/Next.js + TypeScript + semantic HTML/Tailwind/CSS → NestJS modular backend → PostgreSQL + object storage → durable worker/outbox → consent-aware measurement and provider adapters.
Redis, Python, GraphQL, SOAP, Kubernetes, replay, AI, commerce, and booking are activated only when justified. Native and Docker operation remain documented alternatives.
How to apply the standard
Decide the business scope. Complete Chapter 02 and Appendix A; select the primary outcome, audience, modules, ownership, budgets, and legal review.
Build the owner experience. Use Chapters 04–10 to implement approved layouts, CMS workflows, blogs, media, forms, accessibility, and SEO.
Instrument real outcomes. Use Chapters 12–20 to connect the journey from acquisition to accepted lead, qualification, and verified sale without collecting unnecessary private data.
Engineer and prove reliability. Use Chapters 21–30 for backend, database, APIs, security, deployment, monitoring, tests, and recovery.
Transfer and maintain. Use Chapters 31–33 and 65 for costs, handover and maintenance. Chapters 42–65 and Appendices H–L supply the comprehensive expansion; Chapters 66–78 and Appendices M–P complete the final consolidation and release contract.
Non-negotiable release rule
Do not launch with lost or falsely acknowledged leads, unauthorized access, prohibited tracking, leaking secrets, broken primary journeys, or untested recovery. Mark gaps honestly and attach evidence to every critical acceptance requirement.
Using the contents and references
Contents entries and [Rxx] references are clickable. Word fields provide page references; update fields after editing. Source notes distinguish inherited guidance, new official research and account-level checks. Provider rules and legal applicability must be rechecked for each launch.
Version 1.3 | Final consolidated edition
Merged reference • 7 October 2026
This edition consolidates the three supplied versions, preserves their substantive requirements and completes the final operating, measurement and acceptance expansion. It is one implementation reference—not three duplicated documents or a claim that a website has already been built.
78 chapters • 16 appendices • 120 capability entries • 104 final acceptance scenarios in addition to the inherited packs • 128 source references and verification links.
Preserved requirements: manual-only blog/source/trash deletion; attributable protected audit; consent-aware UTM and visitor journeys; explicit browser-location tasks; task-limited chatbot context; stable A/B assignment and honest business conversions. No automatic annual purge is enabled.
Reader guide | Build, operate and verify
Use the same standard for each new website, with a completed site-specific brief. The comprehensive platform delivers its declared foundation and growth capabilities; individual sites activate only approved providers and relevant business modules.
Final does not mean install every industry module or transmit every known visitor field. It means one controlling edition, explicit applicability, working owner journeys, tested contracts and a traceable release decision. Chapter 78 resolves precedence; Appendix P separates documented, implemented, activated and verified states.
Rankings and ad delivery are outcomes of competitive systems. This blueprint provides the implementation and operating controls that the business can own; it does not guarantee first place, profitable spend, worldwide legal compliance or zero maintenance.

01 | The master standard
Purpose: build websites that an owner can operate confidently, that customers can use easily, and whose business results can be measured responsibly. This is an implementation and acceptance standard, not a promise of rankings or an already-built software product.
1.1 Outcomes and definition of completion
Every website built under this standard must have a clear audience, a useful offer, an accessible customer journey, an owner-operated content system, a reliable business backend, and documented operational ownership. Success means the site can attract suitable visitors, help them complete a task, record valid business outcomes, and improve through evidence. A visually polished homepage alone is not completion.
Version 1.1 profile rule: deliver the experiment, attribution, controlled visitor context, contextual chatbot, manual blog-preservation and comprehensive audit capabilities in Chapters 34–41. Earlier Growth/Optional labels remain applicable to unrelated modules and paid AI/replay choices, not a reason to omit these requested capabilities. Public activation remains purpose- and permission-governed.
The reusable baseline is a marketing, content, and lead-generation platform. Commerce, bookings, courses, memberships, marketplaces, and other complex workflows are activated as explicit modules. A site does not become better because every possible feature is installed. It becomes better when required features work together, remain maintainable, and satisfy measurable acceptance criteria.
1.2 Interpret the requirement accurately
SEO, Google Ads, and Meta Ads are separate systems. Google Ads Quality Score is a diagnostic measure, not a direct auction input. Ad auctions also involve factors such as bids, quality, context, and competition. Core Web Vitals contribute to Google's organic ranking systems but good scores do not guarantee first place. [R01] [R02] [R03]
1.3 Normative language
MUST means a release-blocking requirement unless a documented, approved exception exists. SHOULD means the default expectation; alternatives require an architectural decision record. OPTIONAL means implement only when the website brief activates the module and funds its operation. Proposed targets in this document are engineering starting points, not externally guaranteed service levels.
Assign requirement IDs using the categories WEB, CMS, SEO, ADS, EVT, PRIV, API, DB, SEC, OPS, and QA. Each requirement needs an owner, implementation location, automated or manual test, evidence link, and release status. Maintain these records in the repository alongside the project-specific decisions.
1.4 Reuse and change control
Copy this standard into each new project's documentation. Complete the site brief, select modules, approve data collection and budgets, and record deviations before implementation. Keep reusable platform code separate from site-specific branding and content. Update the standard when a release reveals a missing control; avoid silently changing the baseline during an active project.
Version 1.3, dated 7 October 2026, is the final consolidated baseline built from the supplied v1.0, v1.1 and v1.2 documents. Chapters 01–65 and Appendices A–L remain the inherited body, with recorded clarifications. Chapters 66–78 and Appendices M–P add the final operating and acceptance requirements. Appendix M records preservation and changes; current provider and site-specific evidence are required before activation.
Version 1.2 integration
Version 1.2 extends this minimum through Chapters 42–65. Apply the classification and precedence rules in Chapter 42, the 90-entry capability register in Appendix H and the additional evidence scenarios in Appendix I. Scope completeness is a reviewed requirement register, not a ranking or zero-maintenance promise.
02 | Discovery, scope, and feature tiers
2.1 Complete the website brief first
Do not invent testimonials, accreditations, addresses, prices, pass rates, customer counts, certifications, or availability to fill a design. Use a truthful alternative such as an enquiry action until the business supplies verified information. A missing business decision must appear in the decision register, not become a silent developer assumption.
2.2 Three implementation tiers
A feature flag is not a complete deactivation mechanism. Disabling a module must remove its navigation, public routes where appropriate, admin permissions, scheduled jobs, tags, API exposure, and unnecessary resource consumption. Define what happens to retained data and old URLs. Avoid breaking live transactions when disabling a business module.
2.3 Baseline nonfunctional brief
Approve measurable budgets for loading speed, form reliability, data retention, incident response, monthly expenditure, and editorial turnaround. Measure on realistic devices and network conditions, not just an office laptop. Separately define staff availability: an uptime objective without someone responsible for incidents is not an operational plan.
Decide whether the platform is single-site, multi-site, or genuinely multi-tenant. The safest default for independent customers is isolated deployments and databases using a shared codebase. A shared multi-tenant application requires strong tenant isolation, quota enforcement, export/deletion boundaries, and cross-tenant security tests; adding a site_id column alone is not sufficient.
2.4 Discovery deliverables
The approved brief must produce a sitemap, wireframes, content inventory, feature activation matrix, event plan, data-flow inventory, architecture decisions, migration map, and release checklist. Resolve the primary conversion and content owner before selecting dashboards or ad bidding goals. Review the brief with the owner, developer, marketing lead, and privacy/security reviewer.
Version 1.2 integration
Complete every applicable entry in Appendix H and the site-specific decisions in Appendix K. The comprehensive growth platform now includes the detailed automation, scoring, audience, advanced CMS, deliverability and channel contracts; external activation remains permission- and provider-governed.
03 | Reference architecture and dependency strategy
3.1 Recommended implementation
Use a modular architecture with a React/Next.js public website, a React owner console, a NestJS business API, PostgreSQL, object storage, and a background worker. Use strict TypeScript across the web and API layers. Keep Python optional for bounded jobs such as document processing or approved data analysis, not as a second competing business backend.
3.2 Request and publication paths
A public page request reaches the CDN/reverse proxy and then the frontend when no reusable response exists. The frontend reads published content through a stable content interface. Client-side code hydrates only the components that need interaction. A form submits to the business API; the API validates and commits the lead plus an outbox record; a worker sends CRM and communication updates asynchronously.
Publishing content creates an immutable published revision, updates the live revision reference, writes an audit event and outbox record, then invalidates the affected routes and content tags. Navigation, sitemap, related content, and social metadata must refresh consistently. Drafts and authenticated content must never enter the shared public cache. Multi-instance deployments require coordinated cache invalidation and release compatibility. [R28]
3.3 Owned CMS versus an external headless CMS
The reference build uses an owned CMS module behind the NestJS API because full owner workflow control and reusable business integration are central requirements. This is a deliberate investment, not the cheapest first release. A mature CMS may accelerate delivery, provided the team verifies its editing experience, required paid features, export quality, security support, licensing, and integration boundary.
Strapi, for example, documents draft-and-publish capability, but the project must verify the exact edition and workflow entitlements it plans to use. Do not assume every CMS includes visual layout editing, approvals, scheduling, localization, and audit controls in every plan. Choose one content source of truth; do not maintain conflicting page records in two systems. [R32]
3.4 Reduce lock-in without inventing complexity
Define adapters for media storage, email, CRM, analytics destinations, payments, and search. Keep internal domain IDs independent from vendor IDs. Store vendor mappings separately. Export content, media manifests, redirects, form definitions, and business records in documented formats. Perform an export-and-restore exercise before handover.
Do not start with dozens of microservices, Kafka, Kubernetes, GraphQL, SOAP, and multiple databases solely to satisfy a technology checklist. REST is the default public/business interface. GraphQL is an optional read composition layer. SOAP is a legacy integration adapter. Redis and orchestration are enabled only when their benefit outweighs operating cost. The same product must run in a documented simpler profile.
04 | Information architecture and conversion journeys
4.1 Required page and route families
Build task-oriented navigation, not a menu containing every internal department. Public links must resolve to working pages. Avoid hidden critical information, deceptive countdowns, preselected paid extras, obstructive popups, or back-button interference. Downloads and forms must have clear labels and state what the visitor receives.
4.2 Map the journey before designing sections
For each primary audience, define entry source, intent, evidence needed, likely objections, useful next action, and follow-up. A high-intent paid visitor may need price, eligibility, and a short enquiry form. A researching visitor may need a comparison article, guide, and later consultation. These are different journeys, not a single forced funnel.
Use one visually dominant action per important screen region. Provide secondary actions when they serve a distinct need. Track stable action IDs, not whichever button text a marketer writes. Keep the same action identity when text changes; create a new identity when the action's meaning changes.
4.3 Content requirements for every page
Every page record must include a content owner, purpose, target audience, locale, route, template, lifecycle state, primary action, SEO settings, share image, accessibility review status, and next review date. Identify whether the page is intended for search indexing. Marketing-only variants may remain accessible to ads while being deliberately excluded from organic indexing when the SEO plan calls for it.
Use real, authorized proof. Case studies should state their scope and evidence, not imply guaranteed future results. Testimonials require permission and source records. Regulated businesses need additional claim review. Reviews, pricing, and availability shown in structured data must agree with the visible content and actual business state.
4.4 Wireframe and content acceptance
Review desktop, mobile, narrow-width, slow-network, and keyboard journeys. The page must remain understandable before images load. Core navigation and lead capture must not depend on a chat widget or animation. Validate the complete path from the ad promise to the thank-you state and the staff follow-up process, including a failed submission and recovery.
05 | Design system, accessibility, and responsive behavior
5.1 Create a reusable design system
Define tokens for color roles, typography, spacing, containers, borders, radii, shadows, motion, and focus indicators. Expose controlled brand choices to owners, not arbitrary CSS injection. Components must consume tokens consistently so a brand update does not require editing hundreds of pages.
The initial component library must include buttons and links, inputs, select controls, checkboxes, radio groups, validation messages, alerts, dialogs, accordions, tabs where justified, breadcrumbs, navigation, cards, pagination, tables, loading indicators, and empty/error states. Document each component's accessible name, keyboard behavior, disabled/loading state, and analytics hooks.
5.2 Accessibility acceptance standard
Target WCAG 2.2 Level AA for public pages and essential owner workflows. Automated scans are necessary but do not replace keyboard, screen-reader, zoom, and real-user testing. A formal conformance claim needs appropriate evaluation; a passing automated score alone is not that evaluation. [R31]
5.3 Responsive and browser contract
Use content-driven breakpoints and test representative mobile widths, tablets, desktop, touch, mouse, and keyboard. Include Safari/iOS, Chrome/Android, mainstream desktop browsers, and Meta/Instagram in-app browsers where paid campaigns use them. Document supported browser versions at launch rather than freezing an evergreen document to a particular current version.
Render navigation and important content without requiring a user gesture. Ensure forms work with password managers and device autofill. Test long translated text, right-to-left layouts when activated, missing images, slow fonts, and system text enlargement. Reserve dimensions for media to prevent layout jumps.
5.4 Owner-safe customization
Owners can choose approved type scales, color palettes, spacing presets, layouts, button variants, and section backgrounds. The editor should warn about contrast failures, missing labels, and excessive motion. Do not allow theme settings to remove focus outlines or make content inaccessible. Advanced design changes require design-system review and component regression tests.
Brand assets need licensing records, source files, favicon variants, and share-image templates. Store image alt text at usage level when the same image has different meaning in different contexts. Global asset metadata can provide a default, not an inflexible replacement for contextual descriptions.
06 | Frontend engineering and performance
6.1 Route rendering policy
Use server components for suitable content and data access, and small client components for actual interaction. Keep business rules in the API/domain layer, not duplicated between frontend handlers and NestJS. A frontend gateway may simplify cookies and same-origin requests but must not become a second source of truth.
Next.js supports self-hosting, but production operation still requires deployment, cache, proxy, and security decisions. It does not make every page instantly fast. Public environment variables are public; do not place secrets in browser-exposed configuration. Pin and test the chosen framework version rather than copying old cache API examples. [R28] [R29]
6.2 Proposed performance budgets
The Web Vitals thresholds above are Google's published experience thresholds; the other numbers are project starting budgets, not universal standards. Approve realistic exceptions against a measured baseline and business needs. [R04]
6.3 Loading and interaction engineering
Optimize image dimensions, responsive sources, modern formats with suitable fallbacks, and compression. Prioritize the actual above-the-fold hero image; lazy-load below-fold media. Subset and self-host licensed fonts where appropriate, with sensible fallback metrics. Reserve layout space for banners, embeds, and asynchronous blocks.
Defer optional chat, maps, replay, and video players until needed and permitted. Avoid shipping an entire page builder runtime to public visitors. Paginate long lists, debounce search, cancel obsolete requests, and avoid heavy main-thread work on scroll. A consent banner itself must not block the main interaction thread or cover essential controls.
6.4 Resilience and cache correctness
Public content can use a last-known-good published snapshot during a CMS outage. Forms must never show success when the API did not durably accept the submission. Present a retry-safe error and alternative contact route. An analytics failure must not break navigation or lead capture.
Cache keys must include relevant site, locale, content revision, and public variation. Never vary a shared public cache on unbounded personal identifiers. Preview cookies, authenticated responses, and price-sensitive transaction data need explicit handling. Test content updates on every instance; a successful webhook response alone does not prove the public site refreshed.
Final edition integration: Apply Chapter 72 for prerender, hidden-tab and back/forward restoration behavior; do not count speculative requests as actual exposure.
07 | The owner console and autonomy contract
7.1 Owner console navigation
Show plain-language states: saved draft, pending approval, scheduled, live, publication failed, partially refreshed, or rollback available. Never show a green success state when a background publication or integration job is still pending. Distinguish configuration health from business success.
7.2 Autonomy boundary
7.3 Editorial roles and permissions
Provide separate roles for owner, administrator, editor, author, SEO/marketing manager, sales operator, analyst, and integration service account. Small businesses may assign several roles to one person, but permissions remain explicit. Authors cannot publish their own regulated claims where review is required. Analysts receive aggregated or pseudonymous views unless their role justifies identified records.
Destructive actions, domain changes, integration credential changes, permission escalation, and bulk exports require stronger checks and audit. Bulk operations need a preview, affected-record count, and recovery plan. Disable accidental one-click permanent deletion of published content and business records.
7.4 Owner acceptance exercise
Before sign-off, the owner must independently create a page from approved sections, publish an article, update pricing text, replace an image, build a form, receive a lead, create a redirect, change navigation, schedule a promotion, and restore an earlier page revision. A second staff member must repeat critical tasks using the written instructions.
Measure independence by successful tasks, not by the number of settings in an admin menu. Provide inline help, validation, safe defaults, accessible keyboard editing, and an audit history that explains who changed what and when. Document which changes affect live pages immediately and which require approval.
7.5 New owner controls in version 1.1
The owner console must also expose Experiments, Campaign Links, Visitor Context, Conversations, Blog Trash/Restore, Purge Approvals and Audit Center. Each area has scoped permissions, usable failure states and a written operating guide. Detailed workflows and API/database contracts are in Chapters 34–40; the owner demonstrates them under Chapter 41.
Version 1.2 integration
Chapter 44 adds autosave/recovery, undo/redo, conflict resolution, custom collections, imports and coordinated releases. Chapters 45–48 add visual workflows, segments/scores, sender and inbox controls. These require functioning owner screens and safe error states, not configuration fields alone.
08 | Visual builder, media, and publishing workflow
8.1 Block registry
Each block type has a stable key, schema version, display name, editable fields, accessibility rules, default design variants, permitted child blocks, data dependencies, and supported event hooks. Store content as structured data, not arbitrary HTML or executable JavaScript. Components render through an allowlisted registry.
8.2 Content schema and safe editing
A page contains site_id, locale, slug, title, template_key, schema_version, blocks, SEO configuration, workflow state, and revision references. Every block instance has a stable block_id independent of its position. Reordering must not change its identity. Deep duplication into a different page creates new instance IDs while preserving referenced shared content deliberately.
A shared block can be a live reference or a copied snapshot; the editor must show which mode is active. Updating a global banner should display the affected pages before publication. Dependencies such as a deleted price plan, missing form, or unpublished article must block publication or provide an explicit safe fallback.
Unknown block types cannot execute arbitrary code. The renderer should record an operational error and fail safely, using a known fallback where appropriate. Schema migrations need forward compatibility, fixtures, and rollback planning. Old published revisions must remain renderable during application upgrades.
8.3 Publication state machine
Use draft → in_review → approved → scheduled/published → archived, with rejected changes returning to draft. A revision is immutable once published; an edit creates a new revision. Publication atomically changes the live reference and records the actor. Scheduled times are stored in UTC with the chosen editorial timezone retained for display and audit.
Preview requires authorization and an expiring token. Review links must not leak drafts to search engines or analytics. Publication checks include required fields, route uniqueness, broken references, missing alt text, invalid forms, SEO policy, unauthorized embeds, claim review, and stale pricing dates.
Rollback publishes a previously approved compatible revision as a new live action. It does not delete the audit trail or reverse customer transactions. Where a newer schema cannot render an older revision, run a tested content migration rather than promising unconditional rollback.
8.4 Media pipeline
Upload into quarantine, validate actual file type and size, scan where applicable, strip unnecessary metadata, generate approved derivatives, and then mark usable. Treat SVG, HTML, and document uploads as potentially active content. Store private submissions separately from public marketing assets.
Maintain asset checksum, original filename, storage key, MIME type, dimensions, rights, owner, alt defaults, derivatives, and usage references. Replacing an asset must consider caches and accessibility. Deleting an in-use asset requires impact review. Use signed, expiring access for protected downloads; a hidden public URL is not access control.
Version 1.2 integration
Apply Chapter 44 for acknowledged autosave, concurrency, schema evolution, import manifests and coordinated release consistency. It extends these publishing invariants without allowing arbitrary SQL/code or weakening source-blog preservation.
Final edition integration: Preserve required rights/AI provenance while stripping private metadata, as specified in Chapter 66.4.
09 | Blogs, resources, and editorial operations
9.1 Blog content model
The article model includes stable ID, locale, slug, title, summary, body blocks, cover image, author, reviewer where relevant, categories, optional controlled tags, publication date, modified date, source references, related pages, primary action, SEO fields, workflow status, and review due date. Store author profiles and topics as separate entities, not repeated free text.
Support drafts, revisions, preview, scheduling, redirects on slug changes, image captions, code blocks where needed, accessible tables, citations, related content, and an article table of contents for long material. Do not create an indexable page for every low-value tag. Archive and taxonomy pages need a deliberate content and indexing strategy.
9.2 Editorial workflow
Start with audience questions and business expertise, not a target number of AI-generated posts. Each brief specifies search intent, the problem answered, unique contribution, evidence, page relationships, and the next useful action. Subject-matter review verifies important claims before publication. Google recommends people-first content and has policies against scaled content abuse; automation does not exempt a site from those policies. [R06] [R07]
Assign content owners and review dates. Record changes when advice, pricing, availability, or regulations change. Use accurate modification dates for substantive updates rather than making every article look freshly revised. Correct errors transparently where appropriate. Do not publish fabricated citations, invented experience, or copied competitor material.
9.3 Resource library and downloads
For each downloadable item, store title, description, version, file type, size, language, publication/review dates, access policy, landing page, and accessibility status. Provide an accessible HTML summary where useful. Decide whether the resource is public, form-gated, account-protected, or purchased; implement actual access rules accordingly.
A download click is an intent signal. A server authorization or delivery response can provide stronger delivery evidence, but neither proves that the visitor read the file or completed the transfer. Use accurate labels in reporting. Avoid gating essential information or collecting unnecessary personal data merely to inflate lead totals.
9.4 Editorial operating rhythm
Maintain a monthly plan balancing core service pages, comparison/decision resources, customer questions, and timely updates. Review search queries, on-site search failures, sales questions, and content-assisted conversions to identify gaps. Refresh or consolidate weak overlapping pages rather than endlessly adding near-duplicates.
Blog comments are optional. When enabled, add moderation, abuse reporting, spam controls, privacy settings, and link handling. A community feature without moderation ownership must remain disabled. Subscription forms need clear communication preferences and an unsubscribe process independent of account access.
9.5 Manual-only source-content deletion
Posts, drafts, revisions and trashed source content must not expire or be automatically deleted. Review dates, low traffic, AI suggestions, scheduled cleanup and annual reviews create human tasks—not deletion authority. Manual trash/restore and separately approved permanent purge must record the authenticated actor, time, reason, exact object/version and outcome.
Chapter 38 is the controlling content-preservation specification. Generic retention jobs in Chapters 17 and 22 apply only to approved data classes and must exclude protected blog source content. Visitor personal data, backups and derived search/cache copies have separate schedules. A legal removal/hold exception requires accountable review; content preservation cannot override applicable obligations.
Version 1.2 integration
Chapters 43, 49 and 50 add offer/evidence ownership, editorial growth, search-surface and feed controls. Annual review remains a human task. Neither search optimization, imports, storage pressure nor workflow automation may automatically delete source blogs.
10 | Technical SEO and safe migration
10.1 Crawl and index contract
Google's canonicalization guidance treats canonical declarations as signals, not a guarantee that a preferred URL will be selected. Do not use indiscriminate noindex rules as a replacement for a coherent duplicate-content strategy. Structured data eligibility does not guarantee a rich result. [R09] [R08]
10.2 Rendering and routing details
Critical headings, copy, links, metadata, and structured data should be available in a stable rendered page. Test what search and social crawlers receive, including error status codes. Avoid client-side-only redirect logic for permanent moves. A page that appears only after a button click may be difficult to discover through normal linking.
Keep faceted navigation, internal search, calendar permutations, preview links, and tracking parameters from creating uncontrolled URL spaces. Use a route-specific plan rather than blanket blocking that hides useful content. Do not block crawler access to a page and expect it to reliably process that page's noindex directive.
10.3 Multilingual and structured data policy
Language versions need real translated content, reciprocal language relationships where applicable, consistent canonical decisions, and a user-controlled language switcher. Do not redirect solely by inferred location with no escape. Confirm each locale's indexing strategy and use the appropriate hreflang implementation. [R10]
Evaluate Organization, BreadcrumbList, Article/BlogPosting, LocalBusiness, Product/Offer, Event, or other applicable types against current Google support and the real business. FAQ content remains useful, but Google announced that FAQ rich results stopped appearing from 7 May 2026; this blueprint does not promise FAQ rich snippets. [R12]
10.4 Website migration procedure
Inventory existing URLs, traffic, conversions, backlinks, content, downloads, forms, and integrations before redesign. Build an old-to-new URL map with a reason for every removed or consolidated route. Preserve important content and intent where possible. Test redirect targets, chains, query handling, canonicals, sitemaps, and internal links before switching traffic.
Protect staging with access controls and appropriate noindex behavior. At launch, verify production is not accidentally blocked or marked noindex. Retain old URL monitoring, Search Console access, and a rollback plan. Submit and monitor updated sitemaps; investigate material traffic drops by route and search intent rather than assuming a redesign is automatically beneficial.
Version 1.2 integration
Chapter 50 adds detailed route/facet/pagination/sitemap/media/structured-data contracts and release evidence. Validate supported rich-result types and indexing APIs against current official guidance rather than promise automatic indexing.
11 | Organic growth, local discovery, and AI search
11.1 Search strategy beyond technical setup
Technical SEO makes a website eligible and understandable; it does not create useful content or market demand. Map real customer questions to pages with distinct intent. A service page should help someone choose or buy; an educational article should answer a question thoroughly; a comparison page should make fair, substantiated distinctions.
Build topic relationships through useful internal links, accurate author information, evidence, and clear ownership. Avoid keyword stuffing, mass-produced doorway pages, fabricated reviews, and paid-link schemes intended to manipulate ranking. Google's spam policies address these practices, including scaled content abuse. [R07]
11.2 Required growth assets
Do not equate ranking for a low-value query with business success. Evaluate qualified organic visits, successful customer tasks, valid leads, and revenue where measurable. Track search performance separately from paid ads to avoid attributing every improvement to website engineering.
11.3 Local and industry-specific discovery
For a business with real eligible locations or service areas, maintain accurate business identity, contact information, opening/service details, and location pages based on actual operations. Confirm platform eligibility before creating business profiles. Do not invent branches to target extra cities. Use local pages only when they provide genuinely distinct useful information.
Industry modules may need product feeds, real event listings, job postings, accessibility statements, delivery/service-area detail, or regulated claim review. Assign an accountable business reviewer. A schema generator cannot make unsupported claims legitimate or create eligibility where the underlying business does not meet platform requirements.
11.4 AI-assisted discovery and publishing
Use clear, original, well-structured content with accessible supporting evidence. Google's guidance does not require a special AI-only file or schema to appear in its AI search features. Do not sell llms.txt, an “AI score,” or a particular CMS as a guaranteed route to visibility. Such files can be maintained for other documented uses without being represented as a Google ranking requirement. [R11] [R12]
AI can assist with briefs, editing, alt-text suggestions, and content audits, but a human owner remains responsible for correctness, rights, sensitive claims, and publication. Generated content must not auto-publish at scale without review. Preserve source provenance and explicitly mark uncertain statements during drafting rather than converting them into confident claims.
Version 1.2 integration
Use Chapters 43 and 49 for research, offer positioning, content governance, ethical distribution, local operations and AI-search boundaries. Chapter 55 distinguishes search visibility from advertising attribution and authoritative business outcomes.
Final edition integration: Check current generative-search inclusion and report capabilities under Chapter 66; ordinary analytics cannot reveal named AI impressions.
12 | Google Ads readiness and closed-loop conversion
12.1 Website readiness is not auction control
Build landing pages that deliver the ad's promise, work on mobile, explain the offer, disclose important conditions, and allow customers to complete the intended action. Google evaluates destination requirements such as functionality and usability. A good website supports campaigns, but bids, competition, context, creative, audience strategy, and other auction factors also matter. [R05] [R02]
Quality Score can help diagnose expected click-through rate, ad relevance, and landing-page experience. Do not present the displayed score as the auction formula or claim that achieving a particular Core Web Vitals score directly guarantees cheaper clicks. [R01]
12.2 Account and configuration checklist
12.3 Primary versus secondary conversions
For lead-generation sites, pricing views, brochure clicks, and form starts are usually diagnostic events rather than primary bidding goals. A durably accepted valid lead is a stronger signal; a qualified lead or confirmed sale may be the preferred business goal when timely and sufficiently available. Choose the goal deliberately with the campaign owner rather than marking every interaction as a conversion.
Avoid counting the same submission once through a native Google Ads tag and again through an imported GA4 event as separate primary goals. Keep one documented source for each business conversion action and test the account's counting rules. Where transaction identifiers are supported, provide stable identifiers for deduplication according to the relevant conversion action configuration. [R15]
12.4 Enhanced and offline conversion design
Enhanced conversions can use permitted first-party customer information under Google's requirements. Hashing is a transport/matching transformation, not anonymization or permission to send data. Keep normalized identifiers out of GA4 event parameters, the generic dataLayer, and frontend logs. Apply destination-specific transformation only inside a controlled adapter and only when allowed. [R13] [R22]
For a new offline or enhanced-lead integration, use the current supported Google Data Manager API path and verify access and event requirements. Google's 2026 documentation directs these events through Data Manager; the older Google Ads upload path has changed restrictions. Do not begin a new implementation by copying an old UploadClickConversions tutorial without checking current support. [R14] [R16]
The CRM workflow records actual qualification or sale time, currency/value where meaningful, conversion action mapping, available eligible identifiers, and consent state. Queue delivery through an audited adapter. Capture platform diagnostics and retry only eligible failures within current provider rules. A received request, an accepted event, a matched event, and an attributed conversion are different states.
12.5 Paid-traffic release gate
Before increasing spend, submit test leads from representative landing pages, verify duplicate prevention, confirm CRM routing, inspect consent behavior, and reconcile with platform diagnostics. Keep test data and internal traffic separate from optimization where possible. Confirm that the sales team can respond within the agreed service level. Buying more traffic before fixing missed leads or bad conversion signals compounds the problem.
Version 1.2 integration
Chapter 51 expands Google Ads campaign operations, goals, budgets, automated features, native leads and current offline ingestion. Chapter 53 separates reporting, measurement/audience delivery and optional spending-authorized campaign writes.
Final edition integration: Resolve effective campaign goals, including custom-goal behavior, under Chapter 67.3.
13 | Meta Ads, Pixel, and Conversions API
13.1 Operating model
The business must own its Meta business assets, ad account, relevant Facebook/Instagram identities, dataset or pixel configuration, domain administration, and billing/recovery access. Agency or developer access must be delegated and removable. Record the actual asset identifiers and owners in a secure asset register, not in public source code.
Meta's Conversions API is a connection for business event data from supported sources; it complements browser measurement. It does not guarantee full tracking, exact attribution, or permission to bypass user choices. Ad delivery also depends on factors beyond the website, including predicted actions and ad quality. [R17] [R18]
13.2 Event mapping and deduplication contract
The implementation contract must use matching event names and event identifiers for browser/server representations of one action. For a successful form, the server returns the stored conversion event ID; the browser uses that value for its corresponding Meta event. Retries reuse the same event ID. Separate lifecycle events receive separate IDs. Validate the exact Pixel/CAPI field names, dataset configuration, and current deduplication behavior against authenticated official documentation and Events Manager before launch. [R19]
13.3 Server-side event processing
The server adapter sends only the approved event name, action source, actual event time, sanitized source URL, eligible matching data, and relevant custom data. Persist the original event time across retries. Store tokens in a secret manager, not the frontend or CMS text fields. Use an allowlist so a malicious browser cannot request arbitrary event types, values, or destinations.
Consent and eligibility are checked when collecting and again before dispatch. Where available and permitted, platform cookies and click-derived identifiers must follow the provider's rules; do not manufacture them from unrelated data. Do not hash every field indiscriminately. Some identifiers have different handling requirements, which must be tested with provider-specific fixtures.
Do not send sensitive health, financial, children's, or other prohibited information through event names, URLs, product labels, form content, or customer-data fields. An innocent-looking page path can itself disclose sensitive context. Apply a data minimization and platform-policy review before activating events for sensitive businesses. [R20]
13.4 Validation and ongoing operations
Use provider test modes and diagnostics to confirm mapping, event timestamps, duplicate behavior, matching eligibility, and error responses. Test browser-only, server-only, consent-denied, blocker-affected, duplicate-retry, and delayed-delivery cases. Review dataset health and the difference between CRM outcomes and attributed platform results.
Meta API versions, supported event details, account capabilities, and policy restrictions must be checked at implementation time. Several detailed Meta developer pages were rate-limited during preparation of this document; this plan deliberately avoids asserting unverified version-specific deadlines, parameter limits, or historical event-prioritization restrictions. The authenticated integration validation is a release requirement, not an optional later task.
Version 1.2 integration
Chapter 52 expands Meta assets, campaign/creative/destination workflows, native leads, conversion quality and diagnostics. Current account permissions, restrictions and exact API behavior remain explicit verification gates in Appendix L.
Final edition integration: Validate the serialized SDK payload under Chapter 69 before network transmission.
14 | Measurement architecture and event catalogue
14.1 What the system is designed to answer
The measurement system should explain which acquisition sources bring suitable visitors, what content they use, where they encounter friction, which actions become valid leads, and which leads become business outcomes. It should not collect every mouse movement, keystroke, or form value simply because collection is technically possible.
Use three distinct streams: operational/security telemetry needed to run the service; consent-controlled behavioral analytics; and authoritative business records such as leads, orders, bookings, and subscriptions. Define purposes, access, retention, and external destinations separately. “First-party” does not automatically mean exempt from privacy requirements.
14.2 Event dictionary
14.3 Trigger precision
GTM has an Element Visibility trigger; GA4's enhanced scroll measurement is not an exact pricing-section detector. Default enhanced scroll measurement concerns reaching approximately 90% of the page, not visiting a named section. Configure one strategy deliberately and prevent duplicate automatic/custom events. [R23] [R24]
Pause/reset dwell when the document becomes hidden or the route changes. For the pricing rule above, observe a small marker such as the heading or first price card rather than a tall entire section that cannot fit 50% in the viewport. A custom IntersectionObserver can implement continuous dwell. GTM's duration behavior must be checked because its configured visible duration can accumulate; do not describe cumulative time as a continuous second. Store the rule in the event dictionary so analysts know precisely what the event means. [R24]
14.4 Event ownership and versions
Every event definition needs a business owner, trigger, authority, schema version, allowed fields, sensitivity classification, consent purpose, duplicate scope, supported destinations, test cases, and retirement policy. Enforce schemas in the frontend SDK and ingestion API. Unknown events or unexpected fields are rejected or quarantined, not forwarded silently.
Do not register event IDs, user IDs, full URLs, or lead IDs as high-cardinality GA4 custom dimensions. Keep diagnostic details in restricted internal storage. Check current provider collection limits when mapping the catalogue; the internal model should not force every field into every external tool. [R25]
14.5 Extended events and shared context
Appendix F extends this catalogue with acquisition, assignment/exposure, chatbot, location-task, content-deletion and audit-integrity events. Chapter 40 connects their frontend, API and database owners. Use one consent-aware SDK and one authoritative lead workflow for forms and chatbot capture; do not add a duplicate primary conversion merely because a lead came through chat.
15 | Visitor journey and frontend event contract
15.1 The requested journey, implemented honestly
This illustrative sequence assumes the visitor has granted the required analytics permission and any advertising permission needed for the relevant destination. Times are examples, not observations of a real person. Without permission or a reliable identifier, the system intentionally records less information and must not pretend the complete journey is known.
15.2 Identity fields and meanings
Use event_id for one event, page_view_id for one navigation instance, session_id for the defined permitted session, anonymous_id for a permitted browser-level identity, and user_id only for an authenticated internal account where justified. A lead_id identifies a business record, not the anonymous visitor population. Keep internal and external identifiers separate through explicit mappings.
A session timeout, attribution lookback, and retention period are project policy choices; document them. Do not promise cross-device identity from cookies or infer that two people sharing a device are the same person. For sensitive journeys, do not attach pre-form browsing history to an identified lead without an approved purpose, notice, and processing basis.
15.3 Example dataLayer payload
The following is an illustrative contract, not a complete tracking implementation. The application tracking SDK validates the event and checks required permission before pushing it. The GTM container must independently enforce the destination's consent requirements. No email, phone, name, address, form answer, access token, raw query string, or free-text search value belongs in this payload.
{
  "event": "pricing_view",
  "event_schema_version": 1,
  "event_id": "evt_7fa2...",
  "page_view_id": "pv_61b4...",
  "site_id": "site_public_01",
  "page_id": "page_programs_01",
  "route_id": "program_detail",
  "page_path": "/programs/example",
  "locale": "en-IN",
  "content_revision": "rev_0038",
  "section_id": "pricing_primary",
  "visibility_rule": "marker_50pct_continuous_1000ms",
  "consent_policy_version": "policy_2026_10"
}
Use dataLayer.push rather than replacing the shared array after tags have initialized. Treat the dataLayer as a public browser surface. GTM is a distribution/configuration layer, not the database of business truth. Publishing containers requires review, versions, rollback, and separate permissions from content editing. [R26]
15.4 Frontend SDK rules
The SDK should expose typed methods such as trackPageView, trackAction, trackSectionView, trackFormStart, and confirmLeadFromServer. Each method accepts a small approved payload and fills consistent context. It must strip query strings by default, canonicalize approved paths, validate lengths, and refuse unknown properties.
Handle client-side navigation, browser back/forward, and restored pages intentionally. Assign one page-view owner; disable overlapping automatic configuration where it would double count. SPA instrumentation must be tested across route transitions rather than only on the initial document load. [R27]
Do not persist a queue of nonessential behavioral events before permission and replay it after consent. Withdrawal stops future nonessential collection and eligible queued sends according to policy. A failed analytics request must fail quietly without changing the visitor's business action. Browser unload delivery is best effort, not a guarantee.
Final edition integration: Chapter 72 controls lifecycle, cross-tab choice changes, scanners and transport limitations.
16 | Event ingestion, server conversions, and delivery reliability
16.1 Two deliberately different entry paths
Browser behavior enters a schema-validated collector or a consent-gated tag destination. Business conversions originate from a trusted backend transaction. A public collector cannot declare that a purchase happened, that a lead is qualified, or that a payment is worth a given amount. Keep trusted conversion endpoints inaccessible to arbitrary browser assertions.
For a form, the API creates the lead and the conversion outbox record in one database transaction. It returns success only after commit. The response includes the server-generated event_id for any allowed browser representation. Email delivery, CRM availability, or an ad API response must not be required to commit the original valid lead.
16.2 Delivery state model
16.3 Idempotency and deduplication
Use a unique submission idempotency key scoped to the site/form/action and a request fingerprint. Repeating the same key with the same validated request returns the original result; reusing it for different content returns a conflict. Do not use the person's email as a universal deduplication key because the same person can legitimately make multiple enquiries.
The outbox has a stable event ID and unique business-event key. The delivery ledger has a unique pair of event ID and destination configuration. Network delivery is at least once; idempotency and provider deduplication make repeated attempts safe. Do not claim end-to-end exactly-once delivery across third-party APIs.
For Meta browser/server duplicates, use the agreed event name and same event ID for the same action. For Google, use the supported action-specific deduplication mechanism and counting settings. An internal UUID alone does not guarantee that every external platform deduplicates correctly. Contract tests must verify each destination independently. [R19] [R15]
16.4 First-party collection and server-side tagging
A first-party endpoint or server-side GTM may improve control over routing, validation, and data minimization, but it remains subject to the same permission and platform requirements. Do not disguise tracking endpoints or use server-side delivery to circumvent a user's refusal. Server-side GTM is optional and introduces hosting, permissions, and operational responsibilities.
GA4 server-side event collection must follow the currently supported API and session/identity requirements. Measurement Protocol is documented as a supplement to tagging, not a magical replacement for all browser context. Verify the current supported ingestion route when building a new integration and test validation/diagnostics rather than trusting an HTTP success alone. [R30]
16.5 Collection security and quality
Apply payload size limits, rate limits, allowed event lists, origin policy, replay controls where feasible, timestamp skew limits, and bot/internal-traffic classification. Remember that public-site identifiers and origin headers do not make browser events trustworthy business evidence. Sanitize before storage, not only before forwarding.
Observe queue age, suppression counts, rejected payloads, unknown schemas, duplicate rates, destination failures, and business-to-event reconciliation. Keep raw sensitive data out of logs. Provide a restricted replay tool that reuses original identities and checks current eligibility, rather than allowing arbitrary event fabrication.
Final edition integration: Select current Google ingestion/authorization and implement destination-specific final egress checks in Chapter 69.
17 | Consent, privacy, and retention governance
17.1 Privacy is an architectural boundary
Approve a data inventory before enabling tags. For every field and event, record purpose, lawful-processing assessment, sensitivity, source, destinations, access roles, retention, and deletion behavior. Collect the minimum data needed for the stated purpose. A visitor can submit a service enquiry without being forced to accept unrelated advertising tracking.
Determine applicable jurisdictions from the business, audience, and processing arrangement; do not infer the full legal position solely from an IP geolocation lookup. This document proposes a conservative technical baseline and is not a jurisdiction-specific legal opinion. Cookie, advertising, consumer, children's, and sector-specific requirements require qualified review. [R40]
17.2 Consent manager behavior
For Google Consent Mode, configure ad_storage, analytics_storage, ad_user_data, and ad_personalization correctly. Set defaults before measurement commands and apply updates in the correct order. The CMP persists the user's choice; Consent Mode is not itself a consent notice or complete consent-record system. Use the official GTM consent APIs inside templates rather than assuming all gtag and template patterns are interchangeable. [R21]
Basic blocking is the default policy here. Advanced consent-mode behavior, including cookieless pings, is a separate decision requiring legal and technical approval. “Cookieless” does not by itself mean anonymous, risk-free, or automatically permitted. Do not activate URL passthrough or identifier propagation without evaluating where those values may leak.
17.3 Protected fields and prohibited analytics payloads
Never send passwords, authentication tokens, payment credentials, private messages, uploaded documents, full form answers, or directly identifying contact data into generic analytics. GA4 explicitly prohibits sending information Google can recognize as personally identifiable information. Keep eligible enhanced-conversion data in narrowly controlled destination-specific handling, not generic GA4 parameters. [R22] [R13]
Mask or exclude sensitive URL paths and parameters, including email-based links, reset tokens, medical context, application IDs, and customer-specific portal routes. Configure reverse-proxy, CDN, application, error, and analytics logs consistently. A safe frontend event is insufficient when the proxy still stores the full sensitive query string.
17.4 Proposed retention decision matrix
These are starting design ranges for business approval, not legal minimums or maximums. Applicable statutory retention, contractual obligations, investigations, and litigation holds override these examples. Where a law requires a longer period for specified records, implement the legally approved schedule rather than the example below.
17.5 India and other jurisdictional controls
India's Digital Personal Data Protection Rules, 2025 have phased commencement. The official Gazette specifies different effective timelines for different rules; do not assume that every provision was already effective on 6 October 2026 or that a general cookie banner meets every obligation. Confirm the relevant Act commencement notifications, rules, amendments, business applicability, and compliance dates with counsel. A statutory registered “Consent Manager” is not automatically the same thing as a website cookie-management tool. [R41]
For other markets, document applicable consent/opt-out, international transfer, processor-contract, children's-data, and sensitive-data requirements. Where an applicable law requires honoring recognized browser privacy signals, implement and test them. Do not claim universal legal compliance from installing one CMP product.
17.6 Data rights and incidents
Provide a secure process for access, correction, deletion, objection/opt-out, and portability where applicable. Verify requesters proportionately; record deadlines and exceptions. Propagate approved requests to relevant processors and derived stores. Restrict deleted identities from being recreated by a delayed integration retry where that would violate the request.
Maintain an incident plan covering detection, containment, evidence preservation, legal assessment, notification decisions, recovery, and lessons learned. Notification timelines depend on applicable law and contracts. Never hardcode a single global deadline without the legal matrix.
17.7 IP, precise location, chat and content boundaries
Use the field-level schedules and transfer matrix in Appendix F. Raw IP, exact browser coordinates, contact details and conversation text are protected purpose-specific data, not generic analytics context. Browser geolocation requires its own permission/task flow; optional advertising consent is not location permission. Source blogs are preserved until manual action, whereas personal visitor data still expires or is removed under approved rights/retention rules. See Chapters 36–39.
18 | Identity, attribution, and CRM linkage
18.1 Identification without false certainty
An anonymous session is not an identified individual. A browser identifier can disappear, be blocked, or be shared. An authenticated account can provide a stronger link, but household devices, shared accounts, and consent changes still require care. The interface should show the confidence and scope of each linkage rather than presenting all visits as a person's complete history.
Do not use device fingerprinting, probabilistic deanonymization, or purchased identity enrichment as a default feature. Do not claim to reveal the identity of everyone who visits. An identified lead profile contains voluntarily supplied business information; connecting eligible earlier events is a separate controlled processing decision.
18.2 UTM parameters and attribution data model
Allowlist UTMs such as source, medium, campaign, content, and term with length and character controls. Never include names or email addresses in campaign parameters. Where permitted, retain genuine platform click identifiers such as gclid, gbraid/wbraid, or fbclid and provider-specific browser identifiers according to their rules. Do not invent substitutes when values are absent.
18.3 Attribution rules
Implement first eligible touch and last eligible non-direct touch as explicit internal models when useful. Preserve both rather than constantly overwriting acquisition history. Define what counts as direct, how cross-domain journeys are handled, and the lookback period. Do not silently apply an external platform's model to internal business reports.
Separate observed facts from attribution interpretation. “A lead was accepted at 10:01” is a business fact. “Campaign A deserves credit” depends on a rule and available evidence. Platform reports can differ because of identity, consent, windows, modeling, time zones, filtering, and definitions. Reporting should explain these differences, not force totals to match by inventing events.
18.4 CRM linkage and correction
When a valid form is accepted, create an internal lead, then synchronize through a durable adapter. A CRM customer can have multiple enquiries; maintain entity relationships rather than merging everything by email. Record deduplication rules and provide a controlled merge/unmerge process where justified.
A stage correction must be auditable. Do not emit repeated qualified-lead conversions whenever staff edit a note. Use a lifecycle event key that represents the actual qualifying transition. Implement refunds, cancellations, disqualification, and conversion corrections using current destination capabilities and business rules, not by deleting inconvenient history.
18.5 Complete UTM and visitor-context implementation
Chapter 35 expands this model into a campaign link builder, explicit UTM/click-ID fields, sanitation, consent-aware capture, redirect/cross-domain tests, first/last/non-direct touch handling and form/chat/CRM linkage. Chapter 36 adds restricted IP provenance and approximate versus permission-based location; Chapter 37 reuses the same context service for the chatbot. Acquisition facts and attribution interpretation remain separate.
19 | Reporting, experiments, heatmaps, and replay
19.1 Required dashboards
Every dashboard needs definitions, date/timezone, data freshness, data source, filters, and known limitations. Label consented-observation coverage rather than suggesting the analytics denominator includes all visitors. Show counts and rates together; a high conversion rate based on five sessions is not a stable business conclusion.
19.2 Funnel calculations
Define the user unit before calculating a funnel: event, page view, session, permitted browser identity, lead, or customer. For example, a session funnel could measure eligible campaign sessions → pricing-view sessions → form-start sessions → accepted-lead sessions. A lead funnel separately measures accepted leads → qualified leads → paid customers.
Suggested calculations include accepted-lead rate = accepted leads divided by eligible measured sessions, qualification rate = qualified leads divided by accepted leads in the defined cohort, and cost per qualified lead = eligible campaign spend divided by attributed qualified leads under the named model. State time lag and cohort rules; do not mix this week's clicks with all historical sales without explanation.
19.3 Business truth versus platform reports
For a unified owner dashboard, ingest advertising spend and performance through a separate read-only reporting connector. Google Ads reporting uses the Google Ads API; it is distinct from conversion ingestion through Data Manager. Store account, campaign/ad identifiers, date, timezone, currency, metrics, extraction time, and source settings. [R45]
A Meta reporting adapter requires the currently supported Insights interface, permissions, account access, pagination, and asynchronous report handling where applicable; validate these in authenticated official documentation before activation. Use resumable backfills, daily incremental refresh, and a recent-period refresh window for delayed adjustments. Keep report credentials separate from conversion-delivery permissions. [R46]
Never join aggregate campaign rows to individual visitors as though the reporting API revealed person-level ad exposure. Reconcile currency, account timezone, attribution window, breakdowns, and metric definitions before presenting cross-platform totals. Show stale or failed sync status instead of silently displaying old data as current.
PostgreSQL/CRM/payment records are the authority for business outcomes. GA4 and ad platforms provide behavioral and attributed views with their own processing and reporting constraints. Use an internal reporting store or warehouse when detailed joins and longer analysis are justified. Do not turn the production transaction database into an unrestricted analyst query service.
The reconciliation dashboard compares committed outcomes, outbox events, eligible events, attempted deliveries, accepted deliveries, and platform-reported attributed results. Expected differences must be categorized. Received, accepted, matched, and attributed are not interchangeable labels.
19.4 Heatmaps and session replay
Heatmaps and replay are optional diagnostic tools, not a requirement to spy on every user. Activate only after a privacy review, required permission, sampling decision, retention decision, and exclusion tests. Exclude administration, login recovery, payment, sensitive enquiry, private portal, and confidential document views by default.
Mask all input values and sensitive page content. Review actual recordings with synthetic data before launch because default masking alone does not prove that all content is safe. Microsoft Clarity provides masking configuration, but the site owner remains responsible for selecting and testing an appropriate configuration. [R39]
19.5 A/B testing and experimentation standard
Each experiment has a hypothesis, primary metric, guardrail metrics, eligible audience, allocation rule, planned analysis, and stopping criteria. Keep assignment stable where permitted and record actual exposure, not just randomization. Do not change price or contractual terms inconsistently across the funnel.
Avoid declaring a winner from repeated unplanned peeking or tiny samples. Evaluate accessibility, performance, lead quality, refunds, and complaints alongside clicks. Paid landing variants must not cloak materially different content from reviewers or users. Preserve a straightforward rollback and archive the decision with evidence.
19.6 Required A/B testing capability
Chapter 34 makes the complete A/B test workflow a required v1.1 capability: pinned variants, stable assignment, actual exposure, analysis design, sample-quality validation, owner controls, SEO-safe delivery, kill switch and approved promotion/rollback. A live test is not required when traffic or measurement eligibility is insufficient. Replay remains separately reviewed and optional; an experiment must not enable it implicitly.
Version 1.2 integration
Chapters 55–56 add the metric dictionary, connector/warehouse contracts, cost reconciliation, incrementality, research and interactive tools. Chapter 34 remains the detailed site-experiment standard; no low-volume winner is invented.
20 | Forms, lead management, and communications
20.1 Form builder requirements
The owner can create forms from approved field types: text, email, phone, select, radio, checkbox, date, consent, file upload, and conditional/multi-step structures where activated. Each field needs a stable key, label, purpose, validation rules, required state, sensitivity, and retention classification. Form revisions must be immutable once used in submissions.
Use server-side validation as authority and client-side validation for usability. Normalize carefully without changing intended meaning. International phone formats, Unicode names, country-specific postal codes, and assistive technology need real tests. Do not reject valid names because they do not match a narrow English-only pattern.
20.2 Submission transaction
20.3 Anti-spam and safe failure
Use a layered approach: validation, request budgets, honeypot or timing signals where appropriate, reputation/rate controls, and an accessible challenge only when justified. Review false positives. A lead placed in a spam-review bucket should not automatically count as a qualified conversion. Keep a way for a genuine visitor to contact the business when automated checks fail.
Never put a secret CAPTCHA key or integration token in public code. Validate challenge results server-side when such a service is enabled. A CAPTCHA provider outage must have a documented degraded-mode decision, not silently block all genuine enquiries forever.
20.4 CRM workflow
Use explicit stages such as new, assigned, contacted, qualified, proposal, won, lost, spam, and archived. Define stage entry rules, mandatory information, allowed transitions, assignment owner, escalation timers, and reason codes. Support notes, activity history, filters, saved views, exports, and restricted bulk actions.
Define the source of truth for each field when synchronizing an external CRM. Keep provider IDs and synchronization cursors separate. Handle duplicate webhooks, conflicts, replays, revoked credentials, and partial failures. Do not let a temporary CRM outage lose the original lead.
20.5 Communication rules
Transactional acknowledgment is separate from marketing subscription. Store communication preferences by channel and purpose. Marketing messages need the required permission and unsubscribe/suppression process; an enquiry is not automatically permission for every future campaign.
Support editable approved templates with safe variables, preview, localization, and test-send controls. Configure email domain authentication and monitor delivery, bounce, complaint, and suppression signals. Show “notification pending” rather than “customer received email” merely because an API request was accepted. WhatsApp, SMS, chat, and telephony require their own provider rules, costs, access controls, and consent decisions.
Version 1.2 integration
Chapters 45–48 add owner automation, scoring/segmentation, sender authentication/subscription operations and channel-specific inbox/call attribution. All use the same durable lead, policy, suppression and audit services.
21 | Backend modules and business workflows
21.1 Modular monolith as the default
Organize NestJS by business capability, not by a single giant controllers/services folder. Each module owns its domain rules, persistence access, API contract, application services, events, tests, and integration boundary. Shared libraries contain stable primitives, not circular references between every feature.
21.2 Application and domain boundaries
Controllers/resolvers validate the transport contract and delegate to application use cases. Use cases enforce authorization and transactions. Domain services enforce business invariants. Repositories encapsulate persistence. Integration adapters handle provider-specific schemas and errors. Do not put raw provider calls or payment decisions inside a page component.
Use typed request and response models, centralized error mapping, structured redacted logs, request correlation, and tracing. Validate configuration at startup. Refuse to start a production profile with missing required secrets, placeholder domains, or unsafe defaults. Keep health checks informative without exposing credentials or internal network details.
21.3 Background jobs and consistency
Use jobs for notification, CRM synchronization, media processing, publication invalidation, exports, reporting refresh, and conversion delivery. Every job needs an idempotency rule, timeout, retry policy, visibility/lease behavior, dead-letter handling, and a documented owner. A scheduled job running on multiple instances must not create duplicate invoices or publish conflicting revisions.
The database outbox links committed state changes to eventual external effects. Redis may accelerate jobs, but it is not the only durable record of an acknowledged lead. Rebuild eligible work from authoritative records after a queue failure. Use separate resource and persistence policies for disposable caches and queues. [R38]
21.4 Python service boundary
Python remains optional. Appropriate workloads include controlled document extraction, data transformation, model inference, or scheduled reporting that justifies its ecosystem. Define a narrow job contract with input object references, schema version, resource limits, timeout, result schema, and error status.
Python workers must not bypass access rules by editing business tables freely. Use a service account and a bounded API or explicitly owned job tables. Untrusted uploaded documents need isolation, file limits, and safe processing. Model-generated results remain drafts where they affect content, claims, or customer decisions.
21.5 Integration lifecycle
Each integration has an owner, enabled state, supported API version, credential reference, scopes, data map, purpose, rate budget, retry policy, health status, cost owner, and exit/export plan. On disconnect, revoke credentials where possible and stop future jobs. Mark pending work for review instead of discarding it silently.
Expose status such as connected, permission missing, token expired, rate limited, delayed, rejected, and disabled. “Connected” only means authentication succeeded; it does not prove reading, writing, backfill, incremental synchronization, or webhook delivery works. Test those capabilities independently.
Version 1.2 integration
Chapter 63 supplies the integrated work-package/module map for the expanded profile. Policy, domain integrity and source-content preservation are shared services, not duplicate implementations inside each marketing widget.
22 | PostgreSQL schema, integrity, and storage lifecycle
22.1 Data ownership principles
Use PostgreSQL for durable relational and business data. Versioned JSONB is appropriate for flexible content blocks and safe configuration, but critical constraints such as amounts, identity references, workflow state, and uniqueness belong in typed relational structures. Choose one ORM/migration system and commit its migrations; do not mix competing schema mutation mechanisms.
Separate logical domains such as content, business, measurement, and audit through schemas or clear ownership conventions. Isolate sensitive columns and access paths. An analyst role should not automatically read form contact details. A public content query must not expose unpublished revisions or private settings.
22.2 Minimum entity catalogue
22.3 Keys, indexes, and consistency
Prefer opaque stable IDs, timestamptz timestamps, explicit currency codes, and exact numeric or minor-unit representations for money. Never use floating-point arithmetic for financial totals. Keep business occurrence time separate from ingestion and update times. Record created_by/updated_by where relevant and use optimistic concurrency versions for editable records.
Create composite uniqueness for route identity, external mapping identity, idempotency keys, and provider webhook IDs. Add indexes based on actual queries: published route lookup, lead stage/assignee/date, job status/available_at, event occurrence time, and tenant/site scoping. Use keyset pagination for large changing lists. Measure query plans and avoid indexing every JSON property blindly.
Use foreign keys and transactions to protect relationships. For shared multi-site data, make cross-site links impossible through composite scoping constraints where practical. Row-level security can add protection, but PostgreSQL's ownership and privileged-role behavior must be understood. Run application queries under restricted non-owner roles and test isolation, including background jobs and exports. [R33]
22.4 Example relational invariant
The live page is a stable identity; its published revision is a pointer to an immutable revision belonging to that same page and site. Publishing must not allow a revision from another site to become live. Deleting a referenced live revision is prohibited. Archiving a page changes routing and sitemap state deliberately rather than deleting history unexpectedly.
For a lead, commit the submission, lead, and outbox event together. A notification record can fail later without removing the lead. For an order, store a snapshot of line items, amounts, taxes, discounts, and terms used at purchase; later CMS pricing edits do not rewrite historical orders.
22.5 Analytics scale and retention
Start with modest, bounded event storage only when volume fits the primary system's performance budget. Separate ingestion/query credentials and consider time partitions. At growth, move behavioral analytics into a dedicated analytical store/warehouse without changing the business event contract. Do not allow expensive funnel queries to starve lead submissions.
Retention jobs must cover primary tables, object storage, exports, derived reports, integration queues, and backups according to the approved policy. Mark legal holds explicitly. Test deletion and restoration together: restoring a backup must not permanently resurrect records that were lawfully deleted after the backup was taken.
22.6 Protected deletion and context schemas
Extend this catalogue using Chapter 40: experiment versions/variants/assignments/exposures, campaign links, restricted click/IP/location context, conversations/messages, scoped context links, deletion manifests/approvals/operations and independently exported audit batches. Enforce compound site scoping, restricted source-content deletion privileges, manual state transitions and non-cascading audit/tombstone references. Do not add automatic expiry to posts, revisions or trash.
Version 1.2 integration
Chapters 44–55 and 63 add versioned collection/release/workflow/segment/audience/feed/report entities. Appendix J defines representative relational keys and contracts. Each requires scoped integrity, access/retention and migration evidence.
23 | REST, GraphQL, SOAP, and webhook contracts
23.1 Protocol policy
REST with OpenAPI is the default contract for website operations and integrations. Use consistent resource naming, typed schemas, authentication rules, error structures, pagination, idempotency, and versioning. Generate client types from the approved contract and test compatibility in CI.
GraphQL is optional for clients that genuinely need flexible composition. It must call the same application services and authorization rules as REST, not create parallel business logic. Enforce field/object authorization, complexity/depth budgets, pagination limits, batching controls, and appropriate caching. Prefer approved/persisted operations for controlled clients where useful.
SOAP is a separate adapter for a real legacy system with a supplied WSDL/XSD and required authentication policy. Validate messages, disable unsafe external entity resolution, cap XML size/depth, and map faults into internal error categories. Do not expose SOAP simply to claim all API styles are present.
23.2 Core endpoint inventory
