Do not trust a browser-supplied site_id to grant access. Resolve the allowed site from deployment/domain configuration and authenticated membership, and validate any requested scope. Keep administrative and public content access paths separate even when they share the same deployment.
23.3 Form API example
The following shows a representative contract, not deployable code. Contact values are illustrative placeholders; real requests are protected business data and must not enter analytics or unrestricted logs.
{
  "form_revision_id": "formrev_004",
  "fields": {
    "name": "Example visitor",
    "email": "visitor@example.invalid",
    "interest": "consultation"
  },
  "marketing_opt_in": false,
  "attribution_token": "optional_server_validated_reference"
}
{
  "status": "accepted",
  "receipt_id": "receipt_001",
  "conversion_event_id": "evt_lead_001",
  "message": "Your enquiry has been received."
}
The request includes an Idempotency-Key header. A receipt is not an authentication token or a public lead lookup key. Return 201 for a newly accepted resource, an appropriate documented replay response for the same idempotent request, 422 for validation failure, 409 for conflicting edits/keys, 429 for rate limiting, and a safe 5xx error for service failure. Do not expose stack traces or database details.
23.4 Error and webhook rules
Use an error envelope containing code, safe message, request_id, and approved field errors. Preserve detailed diagnostics only in restricted logs. Document timeout, retry, pagination, sorting, filtering, and backward-compatibility behavior. API documentation and fixtures must be generated or tested against actual handlers.
For webhooks, verify the provider's signature using the required original request bytes, enforce replay/timestamp controls where supported, store a unique receipt, and enqueue after durable acceptance. Handle duplicate and out-of-order events. Stripe's webhook guidance illustrates signature verification and robust event handling; apply each provider's exact requirements rather than a generic invented signature format. [R37]
Version 1.2 integration
Extend the API inventory with Chapter 63 and Appendix J. Owner commands require scope, expected versions/idempotency and safe error contracts. Campaign-write and audience credentials are separate from reporting access.
24 | Security, identity, and administrator control
24.1 Security baseline
Use a risk-appropriate OWASP ASVS-based verification plan; a customer portal or transactional site generally warrants stronger assurance than a brochure-only page. Record the selected standard version and verification coverage. Do not label the site certified or penetration-tested without the relevant independent evidence. [R34]
24.2 Authentication and sessions
Use established authentication libraries or a supported identity provider rather than inventing cryptography. Staff MFA is mandatory. Store passwords only with an approved adaptive password hash when passwords are managed locally. Recovery tokens are short-lived, single-use, and never logged. Prevent account enumeration in public recovery responses.
For browser sessions, prefer secure HttpOnly cookies with appropriate SameSite, domain/path, expiry, rotation, and CSRF controls. Do not store long-lived privileged credentials in localStorage. Customer and administrator roles must not be interchangeable. NestJS authentication guidance provides framework integration patterns; the application still must implement its actual authorization policy. [R35]
24.3 Authorization model
Permissions describe actions on scoped resources: read/write/publish content, manage forms, view leads, export PII, manage integrations, change permissions, and perform recovery. Enforce least privilege. An editor may publish content but must not retrieve an ad API secret. A salesperson may view assigned leads without changing site-wide tracking configuration.
Service accounts have explicit scopes, credential rotation, expiry where practical, and accountable owners. No production superuser credential is embedded in application configuration. Temporary support access must be time-limited and audited. Test both horizontal access violations between customers/sites and vertical privilege escalation.
24.4 Platform and application hardening
Use secure response headers, an appropriate CSP, HSTS after verifying HTTPS readiness, clickjacking protection, safe MIME handling, and restrictive referrer policy. Restrict outbound requests from integrations and content importers to prevent SSRF; validate destinations after redirects and block internal-network access where not explicitly required.
Keep secrets in a secret manager or secured deployment mechanism. Only public configuration can be exposed to the frontend. Encrypt and access-control infrastructure state. Treat Kubernetes Secrets and base64 encoding as a representation, not proof of encrypted storage; configure the relevant platform protections. [R36] [R42]
24.5 Audit and emergency controls
Audit logins, permission changes, content publication, lead exports, credential changes, payment/refund actions, data rights actions, and emergency operations. Store actor, action, object, timestamp, request reference, and safe change summary. Avoid copying entire sensitive records into every audit entry.
Provide a controlled ability to disable a compromised integration, revoke sessions, pause a campaign landing destination where appropriate, or switch the public site to a safe maintenance state. The incident runbook must name who can act, how to preserve evidence, and how to restore normal service. Test these actions before a real incident.
24.6 Accountable deletion and protected visitor access
Enforce the separate content-trash, restore, purge-request, purge-approval and purge-execution permissions in Chapter 38. Chapter 39 specifies the audit actor, initiator, approver and executor model, failed-attempt evidence, immutable/independent export, redaction and sensitive-data reveal logging. An ordinary owner/editor cannot erase their own audit history. Context authorization must prevent cross-visitor, cross-conversation and cross-site access.
Version 1.2 integration
Chapter 58 adds identity, API, script/tag, supply-chain and abuse review. Chapter 59 centralizes purpose/rights decisions. Critical source-blog and audit controls remain enforced even when optional marketing features are disabled.
25 | Commerce, bookings, subscriptions, and portals
25.1 Activate business modules deliberately
These capabilities are optional, not unfinished core requirements. Each needs business rules, legal review, capacity planning, customer support ownership, and dedicated tests. An attractive checkout mockup is not a commerce implementation. A calendar widget is not a concurrency-safe booking system.
25.2 Payment safety
Use a supported payment provider's hosted or tokenized collection flow where appropriate, and determine the actual compliance responsibilities for the chosen integration. Do not store card security codes or raw payment credentials. The server computes payable amounts using trusted price and tax rules; browser-supplied totals are untrusted.
A successful browser redirect is not proof of payment. Verify provider events or server-side payment status, validate amount/currency/order association, and move the order through an explicit state machine. Webhooks may arrive more than once or out of order; use unique provider event IDs and idempotent handlers. Reconcile provider settlements and internal orders. [R37]
25.3 Order and refund state models
An example order lifecycle is draft → awaiting_payment → paid → fulfilment_pending → completed, with explicit failed, cancelled, partially_refunded, refunded, and disputed states where relevant. Define which transitions affect inventory, customer access, revenue reporting, and ad conversion adjustments.
Refunds require permission, amount bounds, remaining refundable balance checks, reason codes, provider confirmation, and audit. A full refund must not be implemented as deletion of the original sale. Preserve original financial facts and subsequent adjustments according to the retention policy.
25.4 Booking concurrency and time zones
Store actual instants in UTC and retain the business/location timezone for interpretation. Test daylight-saving transitions when relevant, not only India time. Prevent overbooking through transactional capacity checks or appropriate database constraints/locking, not a browser-disabled button.
Temporary holds expire safely; delayed payment confirmation must resolve against the hold policy. Rescheduling updates reminders and capacity atomically. Staff overrides are explicit and audited. Confirmation pages and emails show unambiguous date, time, timezone, location/meeting link, cancellation terms, and contact path.
25.5 Entitlements and private access
Grant access from verified business state, not a client-side flag. Every protected file/download checks the account's current entitlement, resource scope, expiry, and revocation. Use short-lived signed delivery after authorization. Remove access when policy requires it after cancellation, refund, or account restriction, while preserving legitimate contractual obligations.
Portal analytics must be separately reviewed because authenticated routes can expose sensitive customer context. Disable replay and ad tags by default in private areas. A customer requesting an invoice should not inadvertently transmit invoice details to a marketing platform.
Version 1.2 integration
Chapters 54 and 61 expand catalogue/feed, transaction, booking, subscription, portal and industry lifecycles. Each activated business module needs its full data, customer-obligation, support and acceptance pack.
26 | Search, localization, communications, and optional AI
26.1 Search and discovery
Start with PostgreSQL search or another appropriately scoped engine when it meets the site's needs. Move to a dedicated search service only when relevance, scale, typo tolerance, multilingual features, or operational requirements justify it. Index only published and authorized content. Deleting or unpublishing content must remove it from the relevant search index.
Support useful empty states, accessible result navigation, filters with bounded combinations, pagination, and query-cost limits. Capture search failures using a reviewed query policy. Raw search strings can contain personal or sensitive information and must not be sent unfiltered into analytics.
26.2 Localization
Store locale-specific content, routes, metadata, labels, dates, currencies, and legal copy. Keep translation workflow states and reviewed fallbacks. Do not silently publish machine-translated legal or regulated claims without review. Separate language choice from geography, billing currency, and tax determination.
The editor must show missing translations and relationships between locale versions. Test long text, Unicode, right-to-left where enabled, locale-aware sorting, pluralization, and date/number formatting. Preserve original contact information correctly rather than auto-translating names or addresses.
26.3 Communication and support modules
Newsletter, WhatsApp, SMS, support chat, call tracking, and appointment reminders need independent feature flags, providers, consent policies, suppression rules, costs, and failure states. A click on a telephone or WhatsApp link is not a completed call or conversation; confirmed activity requires an eligible provider integration and an appropriate data policy.
Avoid loading multiple competing chat widgets or marketing SDKs. Use a deferred launcher and consent-aware integration. Define business hours, expected response time, escalation, and fallback channels. Store support records separately from generic analytics and restrict staff access according to purpose.
26.4 AI assistance, optional and bounded
Useful applications include content drafts, approved knowledge search, multilingual assistance, document triage, and lead summarization with an authorized purpose. AI must not invent prices, guarantees, policies, medical/legal advice, or account actions. Ground customer-facing answers in approved content and show a human-contact fallback when evidence is missing.
Use an AI gateway with provider adapters, scoped access, prompt/version records, usage limits, cost budgets, redaction, and evaluation fixtures. Treat uploaded documents and retrieved text as untrusted instructions. Tool calls that change bookings, payments, customer data, or publication require explicit authorization and confirmation appropriate to the action.
Keep model outputs separate from authoritative records until verified. Content suggestions remain drafts; summaries must not silently overwrite original enquiries. Evaluate factuality, privacy leakage, prompt injection, refusal/fallback behavior, latency, and cost before activation. No AI dependency is required for the core website to render or accept leads.
26.5 Optional application capabilities
PWA installation, offline reading, push notifications, communities, directories, job boards, referral programs, loyalty, and gamification require separate requirements. Offline support must not serve private cached content to another user or pretend that an unsubmitted form reached the server. Push permission is requested in context, not immediately on first load.
Enable a module only after its support workflow exists. A public forum without moderation, a referral program without fraud controls, or push notifications without unsubscribe management creates liabilities rather than completeness.
26.6 Required contextual chatbot profile
For version 1.1, the chatbot and shared visitor-context interfaces are required capabilities; a particular generative model remains an implementation choice, with a deterministic FAQ/form and human-contact fallback. Implement Chapter 37 in full. The model sees a task-limited projection, not raw IP, advertising click IDs, precise coordinates or every known customer field. Core forms and pages do not depend on model availability.
Version 1.2 integration
Chapters 48, 56, 61 and 62 expand selected channels, feedback/interactive tools, business modules and interoperable search/AI. Offline/device permissions are deliberate features, never hidden prerequisites for core lead capture.
27 | Repository, folders, and coding standards
27.1 Monorepo layout
The following is the recommended starting structure. Files represent required responsibilities; implementation teams may refine names through an architectural decision, but must preserve separation, tests, and documentation. Do not generate hundreds of empty files simply to imitate the tree.
