  .github/workflows/{ci.yml,release.yml}
27.2 Standard module structure
A backend capability contains module wiring, transport controllers/resolvers, request/response models, application use cases, domain rules, repositories, integration interfaces, and tests. A frontend feature contains views, small components, validation, API hooks, state handling, and tests. Avoid a global utilities folder becoming an undocumented dependency hub.
Keep block schemas and event schemas reusable across editor, renderer, API validation, and test fixtures. Share stable types, not server secrets or database clients with browser bundles. Generated API clients are rebuilt in CI and checked for drift. Site content lives in the CMS; brand configuration and enabled modules are versioned separately.
27.3 Coding and dependency policy
Use strict TypeScript, linting, formatting, type checks, unit tests, and consistent error handling. Prefer small cohesive functions and explicit domain names. Validate external data at runtime even when TypeScript types exist. Define dependency direction and prevent circular imports.
Pin dependency versions through lockfiles, track runtime support windows, review licenses, and record major upgrades. Maintain a software bill of materials and vulnerability triage process. Remove unused packages and abandoned integrations. Do not claim that open source has no maintenance or security costs.
27.4 Developer commands
The repository must implement a predictable command interface such as install, bootstrap, doctor, dev, test, test:e2e, lint, typecheck, build, migrate, seed:demo, backup, restore:verify, and start:prod. These are command requirements, not claims that the repository already exists.
Bootstrap must not overwrite a working .env file, delete a database, rotate secrets without approval, or seed fake business records into production. Doctor should detect missing services, unsafe defaults, incompatible versions, and disabled-module configuration conflicts. Destructive commands require explicit confirmation and environment guards.
27.5 Implementation extensions
Add the concrete paths and module contracts in Chapter 40 to this repository tree. Build context policy/projections, campaign capture, experiment assignment/results, one location request flow, protected chatbot capture, manual content preservation and audit integrity as tested shared services. All additions require real code, schema migrations, API fixtures, owner UI and runbooks—not empty scaffold directories.
Version 1.2 integration
Extend the repository with Chapter 63 and Appendix J. Each path represents implemented responsibilities plus tests/runbooks. A scaffold or mocked provider response is not completion.
28 | Hosting, Docker, Kubernetes, and Terraform
28.1 Supported deployment profiles
Core behavior must not depend on Kubernetes. Provide an implementation-tested native or Compose run path with equivalent business correctness. Disabling Redis requires a supported database-job/cache policy, not commenting out a dependency while leaving code that assumes it exists.
28.2 Network and environment design
Use separate development, staging, and production credentials and data. Production databases are private and inaccessible directly from browsers. Terminate TLS at a managed edge or reverse proxy, control forwarded headers, and restrict administrative surfaces appropriately. Give the public site, admin console, API, and workers only the permissions they need.
Use a CDN for public static assets and safe public pages. Define cache invalidation, signed private delivery, origin protection, and failure behavior. Restrict object-storage permissions; public marketing assets and private customer uploads must not share accidental blanket-read policies. Account for CDN, database, and object-storage egress when planning cost.
28.3 Container requirements
Build multi-stage images, exclude secrets and development artifacts, run as non-root where feasible, use a minimal runtime base, and pin image versions/digests according to policy. Include health checks and clean shutdown handling. Rebuild images for security fixes, not only for feature changes. Docker's guidance supports disciplined image construction; a Dockerfile alone is not a deployment plan. [R43]
Persistent data must not live only inside an ephemeral application container. Keep migrations a controlled release step. Set memory/CPU limits and measure worker concurrency. Ensure shutdown gives in-flight requests/jobs a safe completion or retry path.
28.4 Kubernetes-specific controls
Use readiness, liveness, and startup probes with different purposes. Configure resource requests/limits, disruption budgets where appropriate, rollout strategy, network restrictions, ingress/TLS, secret integration, and autoscaling signals. Queue length may matter more than CPU for workers. Avoid autoscaling that overwhelms PostgreSQL connections.
Prefer a managed PostgreSQL service or a database operation model the team can actually support. Stateful database operators require backup, failover, upgrade, and restore expertise; they are not an automatic simplification. Kubernetes Secrets require appropriate encryption and access controls; base64 representation is not encryption. [R36]
28.5 Terraform and infrastructure ownership
Use reviewed modules for networks, compute, database, storage, CDN/DNS where supported, monitoring, and identity permissions. Keep environments separated through a documented state and access strategy. Review plans before apply; protect production against accidental destroy. Record manual changes and reconcile drift.
Terraform state may contain sensitive values even when outputs are marked sensitive. Use secured remote state, encryption, restricted access, locking where supported, backup, and audited credentials. Do not commit state or secrets into Git. Provider-specific resources are allowed, but document the exit path and data export procedure. [R42]
28.6 Resizing and simplification
Define measurable triggers for scaling: sustained latency, resource saturation, queue lag, database pressure, availability requirement, or team operating burden. Scaling traffic does not automatically require microservices. First examine caching, queries, indexes, media delivery, and third-party scripts.
Test migration from the chosen profile to a simpler one using exports, environment configuration, database restore, and media manifests. Preserve domain, TLS, API contracts, event IDs, consent records, and business state. “Portable” is an acceptance test, not a diagram label.
Version 1.2 integration
Chapters 57 and 60 add domain/account continuity, DNS/TLS, realistic load, safe degradation and operating proof. Native/Docker correctness remains required; orchestration is an optional deployment choice.
29 | CI/CD, monitoring, backups, and incident recovery
29.1 Delivery pipeline
Every change passes formatting/lint, type checks, unit tests, contract tests, dependency/secret scans, build, and relevant integration tests. Changes affecting content rendering also pass representative visual, accessibility, metadata, and tracking tests. Deploy to an isolated preview/staging environment with synthetic data before production approval.
Use immutable versioned artifacts. Record commit, build, migration set, configuration version, and release owner. Approve production separately from code merge where risk justifies it. Keep a release manifest and rollback instructions. A green build without deployed smoke tests is not proof that the website works.
29.2 Safe database and content releases
Use expand-and-contract migrations where old/new code must coexist. Introduce compatible columns or APIs, migrate/backfill safely, deploy consumers, and remove old structures only after verification. Test migrations against production-like data volume and constraints without copying unrestricted personal data into development.
Application rollback and database rollback are different. A previous application version may not understand a new schema or content block. Define compatibility windows and forward-fix options. Back up before risky operations, but do not promise a restore as an instant rollback when it would lose newly accepted business transactions.
29.3 Observability contract
Use structured logs, metrics, and traces with shared correlation identifiers. OpenTelemetry can provide a vendor-neutral instrumentation foundation, but useful dashboards, redaction, alert thresholds, and incident ownership still require implementation. Avoid making logs a second unprotected customer database. [R44]
29.4 Proposed resilience objectives
A small business may approve a target such as 99.9% monthly service availability, a database recovery point objective of 15 minutes, and a recovery time objective of 4 hours. These are illustrative choices, not promises this document can fulfill. The hosting architecture, backups, staffing, and recovery tests must demonstrate the chosen objectives; a nightly-only backup does not support a 15-minute RPO.
Define separate objectives for public content, lead acceptance, CMS editing, analytics freshness, and optional integrations. A CRM outage should not prevent a lead being accepted. A tracking outage should not prevent checkout. Record degraded modes so staff understand what continues and what is delayed.
29.5 Backup and restore runbook
Back up database data and required logs, object assets, configuration references, encryption-key recovery procedures, and infrastructure definitions. Use encryption, restricted accounts, retention limits, and an independent copy appropriate to the risk. Monitor backup completion and test restoration to an isolated environment.
Restore tests verify actual row counts and relationships, published pages, a new test lead, protected downloads, credential reconfiguration, and outbox replay behavior. Reapply deletion/suppression requirements and avoid replaying already completed external payments or notifications. Record measured recovery time and missing steps.
29.6 Incident response
Maintain severity levels, escalation contacts, on-call responsibility, communication templates, access procedures, and recovery steps. Start with business impact: unable to submit leads, payment mismatch, private data exposure, compromised account, or unavailable public pages. Keep time-stamped incident notes and preserve evidence appropriately.
After recovery, document root cause, contributing controls, corrective actions, owners, deadlines, and regression tests. Do not close an incident merely because restarting a container temporarily hides the symptom.
29.7 Independent audit and preservation monitoring
Monitor unexpected blog/revision loss, forbidden source-content cleanup jobs, deletion approvals, raw-data reveals, proxy trust changes, experiment sample quality, chat context leaks and audit export/integrity. Chapter 39 defines separate local durability and remote-export failure behavior. Backup tests must preserve retained blogs and also reapply legitimate later deletion/rights records before restored systems are reopened.
Version 1.2 integration
Chapters 58–60 extend script/security/privacy monitoring, workflow/channel/feed health, database maintenance and recovery. Reapply rights/suppression records after restore and never use cleanup to purge preserved source blogs.
30 | Test strategy and production acceptance gates
30.1 Evidence-based quality
A requirement is complete only when its behavior is implemented and supported by repeatable evidence. Tests must exercise the actual deployed frontend, API, database, queues, and configured integrations. Mocks are useful for isolation but do not prove real provider credentials, permissions, or webhooks work.
30.2 Required visitor tracking test suite
30.3 Core production release gates
The release is blocked by lost or falsely acknowledged leads, unauthorized data access, broken primary journeys, secrets/PII leakage, invalid consent behavior, untested restoration, or unsupported critical business claims. High-severity security issues require an explicitly approved risk decision; they cannot be hidden behind a generic “known issues” label.
Required evidence includes owner task completion, route/redirect crawl, metadata and indexing checks, responsive/accessibility review, performance measurements, realistic load test, form-to-CRM proof, ad event diagnostics, backup restore, role tests, and operational alert tests. Verify all live contact details and business policies with the owner.
30.4 Release sequence
Run a prelaunch content freeze or controlled change window, backup, migration validation, deployment, cache verification, domain/TLS checks, production smoke tests, and a real staff-handled test enquiry. Confirm the production environment has the correct index policy, tracking IDs, credentials, and suppression of test conversions where possible.
Increase paid traffic only after measurement and lead routing are validated. Monitor the first release window and maintain a rollback decision threshold. Continue checking data quality after launch; an ad-platform setting change or CMS edit can break measurement without a code change.
30.5 Honest completion reporting
Use Passed, Failed, Blocked, Not Implemented, and Not Applicable with explanations. Never convert blocked provider access into a passed integration test. A disabled optional module is Not Applicable, not a completed business feature. Report residual risks, accepted exceptions, and named follow-up owners before sign-off.
30.6 Extended release gates
Execute Appendix G in addition to this chapter and Appendix B. Automatic source-blog deletion, cross-visitor chatbot disclosure, fabricated precise location, unauthorized context transfer, invalid experiment winner claims and unaudited destructive operations are release blockers. Inspect actual network payloads, model/tool projections, database permissions, scheduler/storage policies, independent audit evidence and restore results.
Version 1.2 integration
Also execute Chapter 64 and Appendix I. The 192 new scenarios are unexecuted specifications, not results. Use real integration evidence and maintain the same no-loss, no-leak, no-unaudited-deletion release gates.
31 | Roadmap, team responsibilities, and cost model
31.1 Phased delivery
The following is an illustrative plan for a reusable custom platform built by a small experienced team with timely business decisions. Durations overlap and depend heavily on content readiness, integrations, security requirements, and optional modules. Estimate effort against approved scope; these ranges are planning assumptions, not a delivery promise.
A 12–16 week initial programme is only a starting estimation model for the core described here; a sophisticated owned page builder or complex business modules can materially extend it. Reusing a proven CMS may reduce custom development but introduce licensing or workflow tradeoffs. Do not force a date by removing privacy, durability, or security controls silently.
31.2 Responsibility model
The business owner approves offer, content, claims, legal entity details, budgets, and operating processes. A product/UX lead owns journeys and design acceptance. Frontend and backend engineers own their implementation and tests. Marketing owns conversion goals, campaigns, and data interpretation. Operations owns deployment, alerts, backup, and recovery. A privacy/security reviewer owns the relevant risk assessment and release sign-off.
One person may hold multiple roles, but every responsibility must have a named owner and backup. Define a support arrangement after launch. “No developer dependency” cannot mean nobody is responsible for security patches or outages.
31.3 Full cost categories
Do not quote a universal monthly hosting price without traffic, region, availability, storage, and staffing assumptions. Vendor prices must be rechecked when preparing the project budget. This master standard intentionally uses a cost model instead of presenting temporary list prices as permanent facts.
31.4 Sizing worksheet and formulas
Estimate monthly page transfer as visits × page views per visit × average delivered bytes. Estimate event volume as eligible measured sessions × average allowed events per session. Storage includes event size, retention, indexes/replication overhead, media derivatives, and backups; compressed payload size alone is not database footprint.
For illustration, 100,000 visits × 3 page views × 0.8 MB gives approximately 240 GB of page delivery before caching, media, bot, and unit-definition adjustments. If 60,000 sessions are eligible for analytics and emit 12 events each, the observed event volume is 720,000 events. These are hypothetical workload inputs, not predictions or consent-rate targets.
Monthly total = infrastructure + storage/egress + tools/licenses + communication + payment/AI usage + maintenance labor + content/marketing operations + ad spend + taxes/contingency. Keep ad spend separate from website hosting and development. A cheaper server can be more expensive overall if it creates manual support work or lost enquiries.
31.5 Cost control and governance
Set budgets and alerts by environment and provider. Cap nonessential analytics volume, replay sampling, image transformations, exports, and AI usage. Review unused integrations and abandoned test environments. Maintain a decision register for every paid dependency and a documented cancellation/export path.
Quarterly reviews should consider business outcomes, content quality, performance, privacy rules, provider/API changes, vulnerabilities, spend, and owner usability. Update the reusable baseline only through reviewed changes with migration and training notes.
31.6 Version 1.1 scope and estimate update
The expanded experiment, attribution/context, chatbot, content-preservation and audit modules in Chapters 34–41 are additional explicit work. Re-estimate engineering, UX, statistical review, privacy/security validation, integration testing, independent evidence storage, human support and maintenance before committing dates or budgets. No fixed vendor subscription or external AI model is required simply because the capability is in scope.
Version 1.2 integration
Chapter 65 supersedes any assumption that the expanded profile fits the earlier core estimate. Re-estimate selected v1.2 capabilities, provider approvals, specialized review, testing, training and ongoing support explicitly.
32 | Owner handbook, handover, and independence
32.1 Handover package
Do not hand over passwords in a Word document or spreadsheet. Transfer credentials through an approved secure mechanism, rotate temporary access, enable MFA, and revoke unnecessary developer/agency permissions. Confirm the business can recover critical accounts without contacting the original developer.
32.2 Daily and weekly operation
Daily, review new/unassigned leads, failed orders/bookings where enabled, urgent customer messages, integration alerts, and major service issues. Weekly, review campaign/lead quality, content changes, broken links, publication failures, queue health, and unusual data gaps. An owner dashboard should make these tasks visible without requiring log access.
Editors should preview all meaningful changes on mobile and desktop, verify links/downloads, check claims and prices, and confirm the right locale and publication time. Use revision rollback for content mistakes rather than asking a developer to edit the production database.
32.3 Monthly and quarterly operation
Monthly, review stale content, conversion quality, costs, user permissions, retention jobs, provider credentials/scopes, and dependency/security updates. Check forms and notifications with synthetic test data. Quarterly, conduct a restore exercise, access review, API/policy review, privacy review, and a performance/accessibility regression check.
The exact cadence depends on risk and change frequency. Urgent vulnerabilities and incidents are handled promptly rather than waiting for a monthly meeting. Record completed maintenance and unresolved risks in the operating log.
32.4 Independence tests
A second qualified developer, without relying on the original developer's laptop, must be able to install the project, run tests, start the native/Compose profile, deploy to staging, migrate safely, restore a backup, rotate an integration secret, and explain the event pipeline using the repository documentation.
The business owner must be able to create and publish content, change navigation and approved theme settings, receive and manage leads, view understandable reports, export permitted data, and request support. Neither test eliminates maintenance; together they reduce avoidable person-specific dependency.
32.5 Exit and provider replacement
Keep an exit runbook for hosting, CMS, CRM, email, analytics, storage, and payment providers. Define export formats, mapping limitations, required credentials, timing, DNS cutover, rollback, and deletion after migration. Test at least content/media/database portability before initial acceptance.
Preserve customer obligations during provider changes. Existing subscriptions, signed download links, webhook retries, and financial records may require a staged transition. Never cancel a provider first and discover afterward that it held the only copy of business data.
32.6 Owner independence for the added controls
Owner sign-off must include creating a campaign URL, building/previewing/pausing an experiment, inspecting eligible visitor/chat context, receiving a chatbot lead, manually trashing/restoring an article, identifying who performed a deletion and viewing audit integrity. Annual editorial reviews may flag content but never delete it automatically. Use Chapter 41 for the combined walkthrough and Appendix G for evidence.
Version 1.2 integration
Owner handover now includes the Chapter 64 demonstration and Appendix K records: recovery/conflicts, collections/releases, workflow simulation/stop, sender/inbox diagnostics, segments/audiences and accountable content preservation.
33 | Reusable implementation instructions
33.1 Project initiation instructions
Use this chapter as the starting instruction for a development team or coding assistant. It is a specification for work to be performed, not evidence that any work has already been completed.
Build a reusable, owner-operated website platform using this master standard and the approved site brief. Implement the core first, then only the explicitly activated modules. Preserve business-owned accounts, source code, data export, and native/Docker deployment. Do not guarantee rankings, complete visitor identification, unlimited owner code execution, or zero maintenance.
Produce the decisions, sitemap, permission matrix, schema, API contract, block registry, event dictionary, consent/data map, deployment profiles, and acceptance tests before claiming the architecture is complete. List unresolved business inputs explicitly. Use truthful placeholder labels in development only; do not publish invented business facts.
33.2 Implementation rules
Use React/Next.js with TypeScript and semantic HTML/Tailwind/CSS for the public frontend; a React owner console; a NestJS modular business backend; PostgreSQL with reviewed migrations; object storage; and a durable outbox. Python, Redis, GraphQL, SOAP, Kubernetes, and advanced services are optional bounded capabilities selected by the brief.
Build the CMS editing workflow, not just CRUD endpoints. The owner must be able to assemble approved blocks, edit content, preview, submit for review, publish/schedule, inspect status, and roll back safely. The public renderer and editor must use the same versioned schemas. Keep private configuration and secrets out of public data and bundles.
Implement forms as durable business transactions. Keep analytics data separate from contact details. Instrument pricing visibility, downloads, form starts, validation, accepted leads, qualification, and verified sales using the event catalogue. All optional destination dispatch respects purpose/consent and destination policy. Prove duplicate handling and CRM delivery with tests.
33.3 Work-package completion contract
For each module, deliver implemented code, schema/migrations, API documentation, owner UI where needed, tests, fixtures, error/failure states, logs/metrics, configuration, security controls, and a short operating guide. Provide evidence for real integration behavior. Do not mark a module complete because a button exists, a mock returns success, or a provider login works.
Keep changes reviewable and reversible. Never overwrite user secrets, delete production data, silently replace an original repository branch, or deploy without the applicable approval. Document compatibility changes and migration instructions. Use synthetic test data that does not accidentally contact real customers or pollute ad optimization.
33.4 Final delivery checklist
Deliver an architecture decision register, source repository, approved content, test report, vulnerability/risk report, accessibility/performance evidence, tracking validation report, backup restore evidence, owner task sign-off, deployment/rollback guide, cost model, and support ownership. Classify every requirement as passed, failed, blocked, not implemented, or not applicable.
A production-readiness statement must match the evidence. Explicitly list remaining blockers and their consequences. Optional scope can be deferred honestly; a critical missing consent, security, lead-durability, or payment control cannot be described as production-ready merely because the homepage looks complete.
33.5 Version 1.1 implementation instruction
Implement Chapters 34–41 and Appendices F–G as part of the required delivery. Build A/B testing, complete UTM attribution, trusted IP processing, approximate and permission-based geolocation, shared website/chatbot context, manual-only blog deletion and comprehensive protected logging. Apply their contracts to frontend, NestJS APIs, PostgreSQL, workers, owner UI, deployment and tests.
Do not enable unapproved tracking, infer contact identity from an IP, silently request precise location, feed all visitor data to a model, auto-delete old/trashed posts, or let privileged changes proceed without durable audit. Deliver the capabilities even when public activation remains intentionally off. Preserve every original core requirement unless a documented v1.1 rule explicitly strengthens or clarifies it.
Version 1.2 integration
Use this chapter together with Chapters 42–65 and Appendices H–L as the comprehensive implementation instruction. Produce a completed activation register and per-module UI/API/database/policy/event/test/runbook evidence. Keep unmet requirements visible and do not weaken inherited invariants.
34 | A/B testing and controlled experimentation
Required capability in v1.1. The owner must be able to create, preview, approve, run, pause, analyze, and promote website experiments without editing code. Running a live test still requires an approved measurement purpose, enough eligible traffic, and a defensible analysis plan.
34.1 Scope and owner workflow
Implement an Experiments area in the owner console, connected to the same versioned page/block/form registry used by the CMS. Support A/B and A/B/n tests of headlines, hero images, approved layouts, CTA copy or placement, form length, resource offers, and chatbot welcome or qualification flows. A split-URL test is a supported mode, not a different reporting system. Multivariate testing and adaptive bandits are separately activated advanced modes; do not label ordinary uneven traffic allocation a bandit.
The workflow is draft → reviewed → approved → running → paused/completed → decision_recorded → promoted/archived. Archive means retain the experiment definition, decisions, and permitted evidence; it does not delete the tested blog or landing page. Owners select immutable content revisions for every arm, including control. They can duplicate an experiment, preview a variant with an expiring staff-only link, inspect conflicts, and perform an immediate emergency stop. Starting or changing a live test requires a recorded actor and reason.
34.2 Randomization and reliable delivery
Choose the randomization unit deliberately: a permitted browser identity for a public-site test, or an authenticated account for an account-level test. Do not use the IP address, a contact email, device fingerprinting, or a fresh random number on each page render as the assignment unit. A session-level test is possible, but must be explicitly labeled and analyzed as such. A visitor using several devices is not silently deduplicated into one person.
Use a reviewed deterministic bucketing function keyed by site, experiment/version, and the eligible unit, then persist or reproduce the resulting assignment under the approved permission rule. Use separate salts for enrollment and arm assignment; freeze assignment semantics during a running version. Persisted assignments take precedence when an approved traffic ramp occurs. Do not reassign enrolled units merely because weights or content changed. A material treatment or eligibility change creates a new experiment version with a new analysis cohort.
Resolve applicable server-rendered variants before sending HTML to avoid content flashes. Where a browser has not supplied permitted assignment state, serve the documented control. Never hide the whole page while waiting for a testing SDK. For cacheable HTML, use a small, trusted, bounded variant key together with site/locale/content revision, or bypass shared caching; strip untrusted inbound variant headers. Do not cache per-visitor identifiers or Set-Cookie responses into public shared objects. Test SSR/hydration agreement, back/forward navigation, multi-instance cache behavior, and configuration outage fallback.
34.3 Assignment, exposure, and the denominator
Assignment means the allocation service selected an arm. Exposure means the relevant experience was actually delivered/rendered according to the predeclared trigger. Record them separately. A server configuration fetch, preview, preload, or bot request must not masquerade as a human exposure. Page-level exposure follows completed rendering; a component-level visibility metric can additionally use the defined visibility rule. Deduplicate by experiment/version, eligible unit, and the declared exposure scope.
Use an intention-to-treat primary analysis of the pre-treatment eligible randomized population by default. Exposure-based or triggered analyses require a symmetric, treatment-independent trigger and analyst review. Filtering to people who clicked the new button, opened the chatbot, or reached a section changed by the treatment can bias the result. Compare assignment, render, exposure, and outcome counts so missing telemetry is visible. Microsoft documents how sample-ratio mismatch and treatment-dependent filtering can invalidate otherwise convincing results. [R55]
34.4 Metrics and statistical decision contract
Define accepted-lead conversion as eligible randomized units with at least one valid accepted lead divided by eligible randomized units, when the objective is a binary conversion rate. Track leads per unit separately where repeat enquiries matter. Qualified-lead and revenue outcomes require maturation windows and trusted CRM/order events. Do not equate more download clicks with more sales.
Default proposal: a fixed-horizon, two-sided comparison with alpha 0.05 and at least 80% planned power, subject to analyst approval. Record the baseline rate, absolute and relative MDE, required sample per arm, and minimum calendar coverage before starting. These are proposed analysis settings, not universal success thresholds. Use a reviewed statistical library and versioned analysis code. A sequential design is allowed only with a specified sequential method; repeatedly checking an ordinary fixed-horizon p-value does not make it sequentially valid. [R60]
Illustrative sizing calculation: with independent binary outcomes, equal allocation, a baseline of 4%, a target of 5% (one percentage point or 25% relative uplift), alpha 0.05, and power 0.80, a standard normal approximation gives about 6,745 units per arm. At 200 eligible units per day in total, enrollment alone takes about 68 days, before the chosen outcome-maturation period. This is a calculated planning example, not a promise or a universal minimum; clustering, multiple comparisons, traffic loss, and a different model change the result.
Run an A/A validation before the first production programme. Check sample-ratio mismatch, bot/internal-traffic classification, missing outcomes, duplicate events, allocation drift, and consent coverage. Define correction for multiple primary comparisons in A/B/n; keep exploratory segment findings labeled exploratory. Show confidence intervals and effect sizes, not only a green winner badge. Allow inconclusive, harmful, invalid, and insufficient-data outcomes. Low traffic should lead to usability research, interviews, and cautious iterative improvement—not manufactured statistical certainty.
34.5 Guardrails, SEO, and campaign interactions
Guardrails include performance, errors, accessibility, valid-lead quality, complaints, cancellation/refund rate, privacy failures, and price/terms consistency. Automated safety stopping is allowed; automatic promotion based on apparent uplift is disabled by default. A promotion creates an approved CMS publication and invalidation event, with the previous live revision available for rollback. Keep previously issued quotes and accepted contractual prices consistent with server-side business rules.
For search-visible variants, follow Google's testing guidance: avoid cloaking, use canonical signals for closely related split-URL variants, use temporary 302 redirects for temporary split tests, and remove unnecessary test machinery when the experiment ends. Do not identify Googlebot solely to serve a specially favorable offer; the no-cookie fallback should also be available to ordinary visitors without that state. [R47]
Website A/B testing, Google Ads campaign experiments, and Meta ad experiments answer different questions. Sending campaign A only to landing variant A and campaign B only to variant B confounds audience/creative with page effects. Prefer randomization within the same eligible acquisition population, or predeclare stratification. Record experiment/version/variant on eligible lead and conversion references without sending private context. Keep ad optimization settings stable or document changes during the experiment.
34.6 Completion evidence
Deliver the owner workflow, allocation service, versioned config, exposure contract, analysis job, conflict checks, emergency stop, and promotion/rollback tests. A custom implementation needs the same statistical review as a vendor tool. Vendor adapters must allow export of definitions and results; the control experience and lead capture must continue when the vendor is unavailable.
Final edition integration: Experiment exposure uses the activation and visible-document rules in Chapter 72; restored state must not duplicate assignments or leak context.
35 | UTM parameters and end-to-end attribution
Required capability in v1.1. Capture permitted acquisition context once, preserve its meaning across navigation, forms, downloads and chat, and retain the difference between an observed campaign parameter and an inferred attribution decision.
35.1 Parameter dictionary and campaign builder
The owner console must provide a Campaign Link Builder with approved source/medium vocabularies, a campaign registry, destination validation, preview, duplicate detection, and copy/export controls. Store who created or edited each campaign link. Use immutable internal campaign IDs alongside human-readable names so renaming does not destroy joins to spend or leads. Generated links must not contain customer names, email addresses, phone numbers, account IDs, or sensitive audience descriptions.
Google documents the main UTM fields and case-sensitive values. Its current guidance lists utm_creative_format and utm_marketing_tactic but says they are not currently reported in Analytics properties. Therefore, their presence in an internal record must not be represented as automatic GA4 report support. Verify the actual destination map at implementation. [R48]
35.2 Acquisition capture pipeline
At an incoming request, resolve the site from trusted deployment/domain configuration, validate the route, and separate service parameters from acquisition parameters. Under the baseline policy, do not create a persistent marketing profile, identifier, or pre-consent behavioral queue. When the relevant permission is granted, capture only the current allowed acquisition context and mark when it became eligible. There is no retrospective replay of earlier unconsented browsing.
Parse with a URL parser; allowlist names; cap lengths; reject control characters and malformed encoding; redact suspicious identifying content. Preserve valid opaque click IDs without lowercasing, truncating, or double decoding them. Reject invalid values with a non-sensitive reason rather than altering them into plausible-looking IDs. A campaign parameter is untrusted user input, not proof of a paid click or authorization to issue a discount.
Store a bounded, sanitized received field plus normalized campaign mapping and normalization_version. Never retain the full raw query string as an attribution convenience. Referrers are reduced to an approved origin/category or safe route; sensitive paths and query strings are excluded. Hash fragments require an explicit site contract and are not automatically treated as campaign data.
35.3 Browser, backend, and conversion linkage
Create a server-validated acquisition touch and return a scoped opaque attribution reference. Bind it to the permitted session/site and expiry; a signed token alone is not proof of ownership or truthful source data. The form or chatbot submits this reference, not a trusted hidden-field claim of a campaign, IP, qualification, or experiment arm. The backend validates ownership and eligibility before attaching it to a lead.
Maintain first_eligible_touch_id, last_eligible_touch_id, last_eligible_non_direct_touch_id, current_session_touch_id, conversion_touch_id, and attribution_rule_version as appropriate to the chosen internal model. Freeze the lead's conversion-time snapshot; later campaign visits must not rewrite its original acquisition evidence. A subsequent independent enquiry may legitimately have different attribution. Keep source-declared-by-user as a separate field.
A direct return visit does not overwrite a still-eligible non-direct touch under a declared last-non-direct model. Internal menu links must not carry new UTMs; use action_id and placement for internal navigation measurement. Legitimate new external campaign entries can create new touches. Same-site, cross-subdomain, and cross-domain policies must be explicit; avoid repeated touches from redirects, refreshes, and SPA route changes.
35.4 Redirects, Google/Meta, and cross-domain handling
Test ad URLs through HTTP-to-HTTPS, canonical-host, locale, legacy-route, campaign-short-link, and split-test redirects. Preserve eligible approved acquisition parameters to the final landing destination without leaking them to unrelated domains. Do not strip parameters before the permitted measurement stack can consume the sanitized current context. Canonical URLs and sitemaps should not contain acquisition identifiers.
Google's auto-tagging documentation describes GCLID and the need to preserve it through redirects; it also says GA4 prioritizes auto-tagging over manual tagging. The internal warehouse model is separately versioned and must not silently overwrite the platform's interpretation. Confirm current Google/Meta identifier and conversion support in the actual accounts. [R49]
For approved cross-domain journeys, use the provider's supported linker or a short-lived, destination-allowlisted, session-bound transfer reference. Do not append email, raw IP, precise coordinates, chat transcripts, or long-lived identity tokens to links. Do not rewrite every outbound URL. Payment/support-provider returns should not become acquisition sources when the documented unwanted-referral policy excludes them. Test Safari, mobile/in-app browsers, blocked storage, and visitors who decline optional tracking.
Use approved Google and Meta dynamic ad parameters only after validating their exact current macros in the campaign account. Preserve stable campaign/ad identifiers where available; renamed labels may not behave like IDs. The builder must flag unresolved placeholders rather than launch a URL containing literal macro braces. UTMs label a link; they do not reveal an individual ad impression or prove which person saw an ad.
35.5 Reporting, confidence, and operating controls
Join eligible leads, qualified outcomes, and revenue to campaign spending using the stated internal attribution rule, account IDs, currency, and timezone. Show source coverage, unknown/not-collected counts, click-ID availability, and delayed CRM outcomes. Do not claim unknown traffic is direct when collection was refused or blocked. Report observed, declared, inferred, and platform-attributed fields separately.
Add an Attribution Inspector for authorized staff: safe landing route, source/medium/campaign IDs, acquisition and conversion timestamps, consent policy version, assignment references, and destination delivery state. Raw click IDs are restricted; generic analyst exports use safe references. Diagnostics must distinguish missing parameters, rejected input, permission-denied collection, expired lookback, wrong domain, and connector failures.
Test a synthetic tagged visit across multiple pages, a download, a form and a chatbot; then qualify the resulting lead and confirm the same eligible acquisition snapshot remains attached. Test direct returns, multiple external campaigns, malformed/PII-bearing UTMs, campaign renames, revoked consent, duplicate submissions, and CRM correction. Use a designated test campaign and suppress test conversions from production optimization.
36 | IP addresses, location and visitor context
Required capability in v1.1, with field-level access and purpose controls. A network address, an approximate IP-derived region, a browser-provided position, and a voluntarily entered service address are different data types. Never label one as another.
36.1 What can be collected and what cannot be inferred
MaxMind states that its IP-geolocation data is not precise enough to locate an individual, household, or street address; a VPN may locate the exit server rather than the visitor. Approximate coordinates returned by an IP database are not GPS evidence. [R51]
36.2 Trusted IP extraction at the edge
Resolve client IP only on the trusted server side. Lock down origin access behind the intended proxy/CDN, document the exact hop topology, and make the trusted edge remove or overwrite spoofable forwarding headers. In NestJS using the Express adapter, configure trust proxy for the actual approved proxies/subnets rather than blindly enabling trust for all incoming values. Express documents how incorrect trust configuration can allow client-supplied values to be trusted. [R52]
Support validated IPv4 and IPv6 and normalize IPv4-mapped IPv6 consistently. Record ip_source and trusted_proxy_config_version. Test direct-to-origin requests, forged X-Forwarded-For, multiple proxy hops, variable hop paths, and missing edge headers. Never use a browser-submitted ip_address or a third-party public “what is my IP” JavaScript call as the authority. A chatbot backend sees the chat provider's server IP unless a separately authenticated, reviewed source-context contract exists.
Keep raw IP in a separate encrypted/restricted evidence record only when necessary under the approved schedule. Generic events store a request-context reference or coarse region, not the address. For short-window abuse correlation, a keyed rotating HMAC may reduce exposure but remains pseudonymous and must not become a permanent visitor fingerprint. Rate limits combine appropriate service/session budgets with network signals to avoid blocking an entire shared network unnecessarily.
36.3 IP-derived geography and service eligibility
Use a replaceable server-side geolocation adapter: local licensed database or approved provider API. A local database avoids sending each lookup to another provider but still requires updates, licensing, and processing review. Record provider/dataset version, lookup time, country/region/city when available, accuracy_radius_km, and fallback reason. If confidence is absent, store unknown—not an invented confidence score.
Default public behavior may suggest a country or nearby service region under the approved purpose, with a visible correction control. Never use IP-only location as the final authority for delivery eligibility, tax, residency, pricing entitlement, age, or legal compliance. For service routing, prefer a user-selected city/postal area and confirm an actual service address only when needed. Keep precise location out of broad dashboards; aggregate geographic reports and suppress small cohorts.
Location-provider failure must produce unknown or a manual selector, not block the website, lead form or chat. Cache only approved non-sensitive lookup results with bounded freshness. Do not create a long-lived database mapping each IP to a named customer's full browsing history.
36.4 Browser coordinate permission workflow
Provide an explicit “Use my location” action next to a real task such as finding a branch or checking service coverage. Explain why the location is needed and offer city/postal-code entry. Do not request coordinates automatically on page load, simply because the chat opened, or because analytics consent was accepted. An old browser grant does not authorize a new undisclosed purpose.
The Geolocation API requires express browser permission and a secure context. Its permissions policy also affects access from frames. Implement the request on the first-party page where possible; a chatbot can ask the page to show the same location control, but cannot bypass the browser permission model. [R50]
Use getCurrentPosition for a one-time request. Enable high accuracy only when the business task needs it; it does not guarantee a precise result. Choose and test maximumAge and timeout deliberately. Browser permission prompts can outlive the API acquisition timeout, so provide a cancellable UI and ignore late callbacks after cancellation, navigation, or revoked application-purpose permission. Continuous watchPosition is off by default and needs a distinct approved use case and visible stop control.
Store WGS84 latitude/longitude only in the protected location context, with accuracy_m, obtained_at, source=browser_geolocation, purpose, consent/notice reference and expires_at. Validate numeric finiteness, latitude −90..90, longitude −180..180, and positive/valid accuracy; reject stale or malformed data. Do not treat (0,0) as a universal missing-value marker. Browser locations can be simulated or spoofed and are not identity verification.
Handle prompt/not_requested, granted, denied, unavailable, timed_out, cancelled, stale and unsupported states. Do not repeatedly prompt after refusal. Allow the person to continue with a manually selected location or no location when the task allows it. Prefer retaining the derived service-area result rather than coordinates once the task is completed. Reverse geocoding is a separate, approved data disclosure and should happen only when needed.
36.5 Visitor profile and staff visibility
Show an authorized Visitor Context panel with consent scope, first/last eligible visit, current safe page, eligible acquisition, observed journey milestones, experiment arm, device category and approximate location. Mark each value as observed, inferred, declared, verified, expired, unknown or not collected. A profile with anonymous activity must not be labeled an identified customer.
Precise location, raw IP and contact details each require separate permissions and an access log. Mark an authorized reveal/export with actor, purpose and outcome; redact them in ordinary dashboards. Do not put these fields in dataLayer, session-replay recordings, generic error logs, or public JSON. Google documents regional IP and location processing rules; GA4 is not the site's protected raw-IP or precise-location database. [R58]
A name/email/phone is collected through a submitted form, explicit chatbot capture, or an authenticated verified profile—not “fetched” from an IP address. Connecting an earlier eligible journey to a lead requires the approved purpose and evidence. No hidden contact enrichment, account lookup by guessed email, or cross-device inference is part of this standard.
37 | Chatbot and shared visitor-context services
Required chatbot capability in v1.1. The website and chat must use one controlled context system, not two competing trackers. Sharing context means consistent provenance and permission enforcement—not copying all visitor data into the language model or a third-party widget.
37.1 Functional scope and resilient experience
Provide a deferred accessible chat launcher, approved welcome messages, FAQ/knowledge answers, program/product selection, lead capture, resource delivery, appointment requests where activated, human handoff, operating hours, and a useful offline fallback. The main site, pricing, forms and contact details must work without chat. A deterministic FAQ/form flow is the resilience path when the AI model or provider is unavailable.
The owner console must support conversation inbox, assignments, status, tags, approved capture fields, qualification flows, resource links, notification routing, human handoff, multilingual copy, knowledge-source approval, model/provider settings under restricted permissions, token/cost budgets, and test mode. Distinguish saving configuration, publishing a new bot version, and applying that version to new or existing conversations.
Chat states include opened → active → awaiting_customer/awaiting_agent → resolved/closed, with failed and expired states. Messages have stable client/server IDs, sender type, sequence, timestamps and delivery states. WebSocket/SSE reconnection must not duplicate a lead or resend a staff action. Do not expose transcript content in URLs, push-notification previews, or unrestricted monitoring.
37.2 One context service with separate projections
Create a VisitorContextService in NestJS backed by approved session, acquisition, consent and business records. Browser forms and the chat gateway call this service through authenticated/scoped APIs. The service returns a purpose-specific projection and policy_version, not the complete database record. Public configuration must never reveal internal lead IDs or another person's conversation.
A typed context envelope should use context_id, site_id, conversation_id, policy_version, created_at/expires_at and scope. Server lookup must verify the conversation/session binding on every use. References are not credentials; authorization remains necessary. Keep operational chat-session state distinct from analytics identity. A visitor can use support with optional analytics/ads refused; do not retrofit their essential chat session into an undisclosed cross-page tracker.
37.3 Shared capture techniques, without bypassing permission
On permitted chat start, associate the current eligible attribution touch and experiment assignment through server-validated references. Eligible pricing exposure and resource actions can be summarized for an authorized purpose; unknown or unconsented earlier activity remains absent. The chatbot must not claim “I saw you read every price” because a marker appeared on screen.
For location, show the same first-party “Use my location” control used elsewhere. The browser obtains permission, the location service validates and resolves the task, and the bot receives the minimal useful result such as service_area_id or branch options. A denial produces a city/postal-code prompt. Never substitute IP-estimated coordinates and report that browser location permission succeeded.
For names, email and phone, use explicit structured capture fields inside chat. Explain the follow-up purpose and separate marketing permission. Distinguish declared from verified details; use verification only when the business action requires it. Typing an email in free text is not permission to load that email's existing account, order history or earlier conversations. Verify account ownership before accessing private records.
Capture field provenance as source=form/chat/authenticated_profile, collected_at, notice/consent reference, verification_state, and actor/channel. An AI-extracted candidate is a draft until the visitor confirms it or an approved deterministic capture workflow validates it. Do not record every keystroke, autofill value or abandoned draft as a submitted lead.
37.4 Embedded and third-party chat
Prefer same-origin integration or a controlled first-party gateway. For an external iframe, initialize using a short-lived, scoped handshake and a fixed allowlisted origin. Verify message origin, source window, message type and schema; never use wildcard targetOrigin for sensitive communication. MDN documents these postMessage security requirements. [R57]
Do not send long-lived authentication tokens or reusable contact data through URL query parameters. A client-delivered integration token must have narrow scope, short expiry and appropriate binding; exchange it securely and redact it from logs. Gate nonessential third-party widget scripts on the applicable permission. A provider that cannot support the required masking, deletion, consent or export controls fails acceptance.
The chat provider's webhook IP is not the visitor's IP. Provider metadata is accepted only from a verified integration with documented provenance; otherwise use the first-party request context or unknown. Cross-channel handoff to WhatsApp/Telegram is a new channel with its own permissions and identity. Do not place transcripts or precise coordinates into prefilled public links. Signed handoff references expire and do not automatically grant account access.
37.5 Lead capture and conversion correctness
The lead-capture tool validates the explicit submitted fields, idempotency key, conversation ownership, current consent and eligible context references. In one database transaction, it creates the accepted lead, submission/conversation link, safe audit metadata and outbox event. The chatbot says the enquiry was received only after commit. A model-generated phrase such as “lead captured” is not an authoritative event.
Return the stored conversion_event_id to any permitted browser representation. Browser and server destinations reuse the same agreed identity for the same action; a retry must not create a second lead. Distinguish a repeated attempt from a genuinely new enquiry. Link a later form submission to the same case only through explicit workflow evidence—not simply because the contacts match. Qualification and sale remain trusted CRM/business transitions.
Chat opens, messages and suggested-resource clicks are secondary engagement signals. Do not optimize Ads/Meta to every chat message as a lead. Track handoff requested versus an agent actually joined, capture started versus lead committed, and resource suggested versus download clicked/authorized.
37.6 Knowledge freshness and model security
Build the bot knowledge set from approved, published content with site, locale, revision, validity dates and access labels. Query current server-owned pricing/availability where required; do not rely on an old embedding for a live price. Cite public source pages where useful and admit when evidence is missing. Manual unpublishing, trashing or deleting content must invalidate bot retrieval, caches and source links; filter against authoritative current publication state while asynchronous indexes catch up.
Treat website text, uploaded resources, UTM values, user messages and retrieved passages as untrusted data, not instructions to call tools or reveal secrets. Enforce allowed tools, object authorization, bounded arguments, rate limits, confirmation for consequential actions and output validation outside the model. OWASP's prompt-injection guidance supports defense in depth; a prompt alone is not an access-control system. [R56]
Do not give the model raw SQL access, unrestricted URL fetching, production secrets, blog-purge permissions or a tool that disables audit. Log model/prompt/knowledge versions, tool name, safe argument summary, authorization decision, timing, token usage and outcome—not secret-bearing full traces. Provider training/retention and processing-region settings must be reviewed and documented; never promise that a vendor stores nothing without verifying the contract/configuration.
37.7 Privacy, safety and operator evidence
Make AI/human identity clear, provide human contact, and warn against sending unnecessary sensitive details. Mask the entire transcript and chat capture region in session replay. Restrict attachments, validate file types, scan where applicable, and use signed private storage. Conversation exports need authorization, expiry and audit. Privacy requests must cover transcripts, extracted contacts, summaries, attachments, search indexes and provider copies under the approved policy.
Test cross-visitor leakage, guessed conversation IDs, shared-device logout/reset, expired context, consent withdrawal during a conversation, location refusal, incorrect AI extraction, tool denial, provider outage, reconnection and duplicate lead retries. Evidence must show that forms and chat use the same acquisition and conversion rules while keeping prohibited data out of analytics and model context.
38 | Manual-only blog deletion and content preservation
Mandatory invariant: blog posts, drafts, published revisions, editorial history and content-owned assets must not be automatically deleted because of age, inactivity, low traffic, storage thresholds, AI recommendations, a yearly schedule, or time spent in trash. Only an authorized, attributable human deletion workflow can approve removal of source content, subject to legal obligations.
38.1 Interpretation and lifecycle separation
The request's “anually” is interpreted as “manually,” not automatic annual deletion. Annual review can create reminders and a candidate list, but cannot change publication state or delete content on its own. An annual-only human deletion window may be configured only after the owner explicitly confirms that separate scheduling requirement; it must not obstruct legally required action. No yearly purge is enabled in this standard.
Separate three concepts: editorial workflow state (draft/in_review/approved), publication state (unpublished/published/archived), and deletion state (active/trashed/purge_pending/purged). Unpublishing removes public availability; archiving retains the source; moving to trash is a reversible manual action; hard purge is a separately authorized permanent removal. A review_due_at date is not expires_at. A scheduled publication is not scheduled deletion.
An automated expiry rule for a dated offer may be separately approved to stop displaying an invalid claim, but it must retain the post and audit the rule execution. Ordinary posts do not auto-unpublish on age. Search/retrieval indexes and cached renderings are derived copies: they may be rebuilt or invalidated without deleting the source post.
38.2 Owner permissions and manual trash action
Separate content.edit, content.publish, content.unpublish, content.trash, content.restore, content.purge_request, content.purge_approve, content.purge_execute and audit.read. Roles are explicit; an author cannot gain purge rights by owning a post. General integrations, retention jobs and chatbot/AI service accounts receive no source-content deletion authority.
For manual trash, require authenticated staff identity, site/object permission, expected record version, a human-readable reason or approved reason code, impact preview and explicit confirmation. Show affected routes, translations, active experiments, linked resources, menus, campaigns and bot knowledge. Confirm active campaigns and customer obligations before removing destinations. Commit the state change, deletion metadata, audit and invalidation outbox together.
Store deleted_at, deleted_by_actor_id, deletion_reason, previous_publication_state, expected_revision/version, request_id and operation_id. The trusted backend derives the actor from authentication; it never trusts a submitted deleted_by field. A failed attempt records failure without pretending a deletion occurred. The owner receives a safe notification and can inspect who did what and why.
38.3 Trash has no automatic expiry
The trash screen supports search, filters, deletion reason, actor, timestamps, dependency view, restore preview and authorized restore. There is no “empty trash after 30 days” default, hidden CMS cron, database TTL, cloud lifecycle rule or generic cleanup service that removes retained posts or their source revisions. Disable or replace such CMS/plugin behavior before acceptance.
A manual restore validates permissions, legal holds, slug conflicts, schema compatibility and content dependencies. Restore to a safe unpublished state by default; republication is a separate approved action. Record restored_by, restored_at, source deletion operation and revision. Remove/reconcile old redirects and invalidate the necessary caches before showing restored content as live.
Slugs remain reserved while a post is in trash unless an explicit, audited reassignment is approved. A deleted author's account, category or media folder must not cascade-delete articles. Use restrictive foreign keys and controlled detachment/reassignment where needed. Shared assets are retained while referenced by active, trashed or recoverable source revisions according to this preservation policy.
38.4 Exceptional permanent purge
Hard purge is disabled for ordinary editors and absent from public APIs. The default is a two-person workflow: request → impact/hold check → independent approval → fresh privileged confirmation → execution → verification. Requester and approver must differ. A genuinely single-owner organization needs an explicitly approved exception with step-up authentication, clear irreversible warning and independent audit storage; do not silently weaken the default.
Approval binds to site, exact post/revision manifest, reason, permitted action and expiry. Any changed manifest, restored post, new hold, changed permission or expired approval invalidates it. An execution worker may carry out an already human-authorized bounded operation with an auditable service identity; it cannot decide which posts to delete. Recheck authorization, hold and manifest at execution. Retries reuse operation_id and never expand the deletion set.
A purge transaction first records the approved destructive action and a minimal tombstone/manifest; it then deletes the allowed source records and records outcomes through durable state. External storage deletion and index/CDN invalidation use resumable child jobs. Track requested, database_removed, assets_removed, derived_copies_removed, backup_expiry_pending, completed or failed distinctly. Do not claim instantaneous deletion from every backup or third-party copy.
38.5 Database, storage, SEO and backup controls
The normal application database role must not have DELETE or TRUNCATE authority over protected source-content tables. Use narrow update rights and reviewed transition procedures/guards so an arbitrary content.edit request cannot set deleted_at or purge state. Only a separated, short-lived, audited purge execution path can remove the approved manifest. Database privileges are an enforcement layer, but database owners and elevated roles need independent governance and monitoring. [R59]
Protect content/media buckets from indiscriminate lifecycle expiration. Inventory CMS plugins, scheduled tasks, ORM cascade rules, storage lifecycle policies, admin APIs, migration scripts and infrastructure destroy operations. Destructive deployments/imports must never reset content to demo data. Monitor unexpected reductions in post/revision counts and periodically test restore from a real backup copy.
When a URL is deliberately removed, choose a relevant redirect only when there is a genuine replacement; otherwise use a deliberate 404/410 policy and remove the URL from sitemap/navigation/search. Do not redirect all deleted posts to the homepage. Remove obsolete content from chatbot retrieval, summaries/caches, and public feeds. Content revisions retained privately must remain inaccessible to public APIs.
Backups retain their approved rolling schedule; “never auto-delete blogs” does not mean retain every backup forever. Source content remains until manual action. After a lawful purge, a retained backup must not reintroduce the removed content on restore: replay the independent deletion/suppression ledger before reopening access. Document when expired backups cease to contain the data.
38.6 Legal exceptions and proof
Manual editorial retention does not override a valid legal removal obligation, privacy request, security containment, licensed-content takedown or litigation hold. Route those through an authorized exception workflow with accountable review, scope, deadline and minimal audit evidence. Do not retain sensitive deleted text inside an immutable audit record merely to prove it was removed. Maintain content version hashes and safe metadata only as justified; even hashes/identifiers require privacy review.
Acceptance must advance the application clock beyond one year and run all cleanup/retention jobs: active and trashed posts remain intact. Test unauthorized DELETE/TRUNCATE, bulk-delete preview, approval expiry, requester=approver denial, concurrent edits, legal holds, shared assets, actor deactivation, interrupted purge retries, restore conflicts and backup resurrection prevention. A note in the owner handbook alone is not enforcement.
39 | Audit trails and comprehensive system logging
“Everything logged” means complete coverage of defined administrative actions, trusted business transitions, security events and permitted visitor behavior. It does not mean recording passwords, payment secrets, raw form answers, private transcripts or precise coordinates into unrestricted logs.
39.1 Separate log streams and their purposes
OWASP recommends deliberate event selection, protected logging, and excluding sensitive data. This blueprint's coverage catalogue and retention choices are project design requirements; “more logs” is not automatically safer or more useful. [R53]
39.2 Mandatory coverage catalogue
Audit content create/edit/revision/publish/unpublish/archive/trash/restore/purge; media replacement and access changes; menus/redirects/SEO edits; form-schema and validation changes; campaign-link changes; experiment draft/approval/start/ramp/pause/promotion; chatbot flow/knowledge/model configuration; integration credentials by reference; tag/CMP configuration publication; and feature activation/deactivation.
Also audit logins and important authentication failures, MFA/recovery changes, user invitations/removal, role grants/revocations, impersonation/support access, lead assignment/qualification/merge/unmerge, payment/refund actions, booking overrides, privacy requests, consent-policy changes, exports, protected IP/location/transcript reveals, audit-log searches/exports, backups/restores, migration approvals and emergency access.
For each action, define success, denial, failure and cancellation where meaningful. Public high-volume requests may use bounded operational logging; privileged state changes must never disappear into a sampling rule. Retention jobs log policy version, eligible counts, excluded legal holds, data class and outcome, but must not select protected blog source content for automatic deletion.
39.3 Audit event schema and actor attribution
Every critical entry includes audit_id, site_id, occurred_at and recorded_at in UTC, action, resource_type/resource_id, resource_version, outcome, actor_type, actor_id, authenticated identity/session reference, request_id, operation_id, reason_code and safe change summary. Include initiator_actor_id, approver_actor_id and executor_service_id where a human-approved asynchronous action is executed by a worker. Capture permission_policy_version and application/config version.
Use a non-secret correlation reference for authenticated sessions, not a live bearer token or session cookie. A human identity remains attributable after staff deactivation through a restricted minimal actor registry; do not cascade-delete audit entries when removing a staff account. Preserve a justified actor display snapshot or recoverable mapping under its own retention policy. Site labels, resource titles and reasons can themselves contain sensitive data, so sanitize and minimize them.
Before/after values are allowed only for reviewed non-sensitive fields. For other fields record changed field keys, version references and protected evidence references. Never store a complete contact or transcript snapshot in every diff. IP and location, when justified, live in a separate restricted request-context record. UI readers see a redacted change history by default.
39.4 Atomicity, availability and failed attempts
For content publication, role changes, deletion and other critical mutations, commit the domain change, local durable audit entry and outbox together. If local audit persistence fails, the protected mutation must fail rather than proceed invisibly. A remote log-export outage should not block a valid lead if the local transactional evidence was durably committed; queue and reconcile the export.
Denied/failed attempts cannot rely solely on a transaction that rolls back. Write their safe security/audit event through a separately committed path or independent sink. Record attempted action and failure reason without falsely recording state change. Correlate attempt and completion using operation_id. Do not claim exactly-once external logging; implement idempotent export and deduplicate retries.
Monitor local durability, export lag, sequence gaps, rejected log schemas, disk/storage pressure and reconciliation counts. Set bounded buffering and an escalation policy. Pause high-risk destructive administration when required audit guarantees are unavailable. Keep ordinary public reading and safe business capture available whenever their own integrity requirements can still be met.
39.5 Tamper resistance and independent evidence
The application write path can append approved audit records but cannot edit/delete old ones. Separate read, append, retention and break-glass roles; ordinary owners/admins cannot erase their own history through the CMS. Record audit configuration changes and attempted log deletion in an independently protected stream. Use TLS, encryption, restricted keys and independent backup/export credentials.
Export audit batches with ordered manifests, sequence numbers, content digests and signed checkpoints to independent storage. A hash chain only detects some changes when a trusted checkpoint survives elsewhere; it does not stop a privileged attacker rewriting the whole database and chain. Serialize or otherwise define ordering per stream/site; do not invent a global transaction order from unsynchronized clocks. Verification jobs must detect gaps and altered manifests.
For higher assurance, use a reviewed WORM/object-lock backend with a justified retention period. AWS documents version-level Object Lock, governance/compliance differences and the fact that delete markers can still obscure a locked version. Therefore, monitor version manifests and access policy as well as a bucket's “Object Lock enabled” flag. Avoid unreviewed indefinite locks on personal data. Equivalent alternative storage must pass the same tamper/restore tests. [R54]
39.6 Redaction, investigation UI and alerts
Redact at the producer and collector: Authorization/Cookie headers, tokens, passwords, keys, payment fields, full URL queries, message bodies, form values and sensitive uploaded filenames. Use allowlisted route templates and error codes; sanitize line breaks/control characters to prevent log injection. Telemetry for third-party AI/tool calls must follow the same rule. Security investigators can use a controlled evidence store when raw data is necessary, not turn off redaction globally.
Provide an Audit Center with actor, action, object, site, time, outcome, operation and reason filters; version comparison; deletion/approval timeline; integrity status; and authorized export. Default search is paginated and redacted. Audit the act of viewing/exporting sensitive records, without generating recursive audit-of-audit loops. Exports use expiry, access checks and injection-safe CSV/document formats.
Alert on blog purge requests/completions, unexpected source-content loss, role escalation, bulk exports, tracking-policy changes, forbidden tags/fields, geo-context exposure, cross-visitor chat access, integration-secret changes, audit export lag and signature/checkpoint failures. Notifications contain safe metadata and an authenticated admin link, not the IP, coordinates, transcript or deleted article body.
39.7 Retention, legal holds and log completeness
Define retention per stream and data field; raw IP, transcripts, behavioral events and minimal audit metadata need not share the same period. Retention expiry is policy-controlled and itself logged through a minimal durable disposal record. A business preference to preserve blog source content does not justify retaining personal visitor data forever. Apply legal holds with a named reviewer and documented release.
Publish a log-coverage register showing required action, emitter, storage, transaction boundary, retention, access roles, alert and test. Reconcile source business-operation counts with audit/outbox/export manifests. The dashboard reports healthy, delayed, incomplete, integrity_failed or unavailable—not a false promise that distributed logs can never be lost. Release requires evidence for each critical action and a recovery exercise.
40 | Integrated implementation contracts: frontend, API and database
40.1 Module and data-flow integration
Add experimentation, attribution, visitor-context, geolocation, chat, content-preservation and audit-integrity modules to the existing NestJS modular backend. They use the existing identity/privacy policy layer, not duplicated permission checks in each widget. Keep PostgreSQL as the durable source of truth, object storage for protected assets/evidence, and outbox workers for asynchronous effects. Redis remains optional for bounded caches/coordination; Python may run reviewed statistical jobs behind a versioned contract but is not mandatory for serving pages.
The allowed journey is: trusted edge request → purpose/consent decision → eligible acquisition/context → approved variant render → permitted behavior events → explicit form/chat submission → transactional lead/audit/outbox → CRM qualification → eligible Ads/Meta feedback. Precise location branches into a protected location service and returns a minimal service-area result. It does not join every outgoing event. Blog preservation and administrative audit are enforced regardless of analytics consent.
40.2 Frontend and owner-console additions
Coordinates must not be persisted in browser localStorage by default. Operational chat authentication uses the existing secure session mechanism where possible; marketing identity remains separately permissioned. Every frontend state includes loading, unavailable, refused, expired, validation failed, success and retry where relevant. A “connected” badge is not proof that a provider receives correct data.
40.3 Database additions and integrity
Add protected fields to posts: deletion_state, deleted_at, deleted_by_actor_id, deletion_reason, restored_at, restored_by_actor_id and version. Keep these separate from published_revision_id and review_due_at. Do not attach an expires_at TTL to a source post, revision or trash row. An audit reference to a deleted object must survive without a cascading FK; use the minimal tombstone identity and justified actor mapping.
Use compound site/resource keys to prevent cross-tenant joins; apply row/object authorization and restricted database roles. Encrypt sensitive columns with managed keys and rotation procedures where justified. A coordinate-capable PostGIS column is optional for spatial queries; validate SRID 4326 and longitude/latitude ordering. Do not add PostGIS merely to store an approximate country code.
40.4 REST endpoint additions
The precise route names can change through an architecture decision, but the use-case and authorization boundaries cannot disappear. Keep public collectors separate from administrative mutation. The same domain authorization must apply to any selected GraphQL mutations or SOAP adapters. Include request schemas, response states, idempotency, 403/409/422 behavior and failure examples in OpenAPI.
40.5 Repository additions
apps/web/src/components/experiments/ExperimentBoundary.tsx
apps/web/src/components/location/LocationRequestButton.tsx
apps/web/src/components/chat/{ChatLauncher,ChatCaptureForm}.tsx

apps/admin/src/features/{audit-center,retention-policies}/
apps/api/src/modules/experimentation/{domain,application,infra}/
apps/api/src/modules/attribution/{domain,application,infra}/
apps/api/src/modules/visitor-context/{policy,projections,service}/
apps/api/src/modules/geolocation/{providers,validators,service}/
apps/api/src/modules/chat/{gateway,capture,tools,knowledge}/

These paths extend Chapter 27 and represent real responsibilities, not a request to create empty files. Implement shared schema fixtures, migration tests, policy tests, and owner task guides alongside each module. The approved-purge worker is not a scheduled content-retention worker; it processes only a valid human-authorized manifest.
40.6 Configuration and deployment controls
Version site capabilities separately from runtime permission state. Provide approved flags for experimentation, chat, approximate-location enrichment, browser-location task, replay and destination delivery. Default deny at the field/destination level; content preservation and critical audit are not optional marketing flags. Keep an explicit BLOG_AUTO_DELETE_ENABLED=false invariant enforced by startup validation and tests rather than a casually editable CMS switch.
Settings include approved attribution lookback, campaign naming version, consent policy version, location timeout/maximum age, location/raw-IP retention, chat retention, experiment enrollment/analysis mode, audit export interval/retention, and purge approval requirements. No secret belongs in public environment variables. Do not use “disable Redis” to disable audit durability or conversion outbox.
Native and Docker profiles must expose equivalent behavior; Kubernetes/Terraform profiles add the relevant network restrictions, durable storage, secret references and independent audit export, not different business semantics. Back up content, permission configuration, experiment/campaign definitions, required ledgers and protected conversation state under their schedules. Exercise migration with real-compatible revisions before launch.
41 | Integrated delivery, owner runbooks and acceptance
